\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}n\cdot\Big((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u_{0}\cdot\nabla)\nabla\rho_{0}+(\nabla u_{0})^{T}\nabla\rho_{0}\Big)+U_{0}n\cdot\nabla\rho_{1}

\displaystyle+(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}):\Big(\tfrac{1}{4}A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})-d\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{1})-d\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}^{2}\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{d+1}{d-1}U_{0}(n\cdot\nabla\rho_{0})\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}U_{0}\rho_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{d+2}{2}\rho_{0}\Big(\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)^{2}-\tfrac{2}{d(d+2)}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2})\Big).

The equation in (
2.3
) for
\rho_{0}
entails that the first right-hand side term vanishes as

(3.12)

(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u_{0}\cdot\nabla)\nabla\rho_{0}+(\nabla u_{0})^{T}\nabla\rho_{0}\,=\,\nabla(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u_{0}\cdot\nabla)\rho_{0}\,=\,0.

Now appealing to (
3.7
),
the second term reads

\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}):\Big(\tfrac{1}{4}A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})-d\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{1})-d\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}^{2}\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\Big)$\mbox{}\hfil\\
\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{2d}\Delta_{n}\bigg((n\otimes n):\Big(\tfrac{1}{4}A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})-d\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{1})-d\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}^{2}\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\Big)\operatorname{Id}\bigg).

Using (
3.8
), we further get for the third term,

\tfrac{d+1}{d-1}U_{0}(n\cdot\nabla\rho_{0})\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)\\
=-\tfrac{U_{0}}{3(d-1)}\Delta_{n}\bigg((\nabla\rho_{0})\cdot\Big(n\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)+\tfrac{4}{d-1}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})n\Big)\bigg),

and for the fourth term,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}U_{0}\rho_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)=-\tfrac{U_{0}}{6(d+1)}\Delta_{n}\bigg(\rho_{0}\,{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\Big(n\big((n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)\bigg).

Finally, using (
3.9
) for the last term, the explicit form (
3.2
) for
g_{2}
follows.

Step
2. Proof of item (i).

In view of the explicit solution for the equation for
g_{1}
obtained in Step 1, cf. (
3.1
),
the elastic stress
\sigma_{1}[g_{1}]
defined in (
1.3
) takes the form

\sigma_{1}[g_{1}]\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda\theta\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)\operatorname{D}(u_{0})(n\otimes n)\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}n\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\theta\,\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},\nabla u_{0}],

where we have used that integrals of monomials of odd degree on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
vanish.
By (
3.11
), this actually means

\sigma_{1}[g_{1}]\,=\,\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\lambda\theta\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\rho_{0}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\theta\,\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},\nabla u_{0}].

In the Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
, the system (
2.3
) then becomes

(3.13)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-{\operatorname{div}}\big((1+c_{0}\rho_{0})2\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)+\nabla p_{0}=h,\\[2.84526pt]
(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u_{0}\cdot\nabla)\rho_{0}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u_{0})=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{0}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\rho_{0}|_{t=0}=\rho^{\circ},\end{array}\right.

where we have set for shortness
c_{0}:=\lambda\tfrac{\omega_{d}(\theta+2)}{2d(d+2)}
.
Surprisingly, we could not find a reference for this natural system.
We establish the well-posedness and the propagation of regularity for this system, which we believe to be of independent interest, and we split the proof into five further substeps. Note that the propagation of regularity requires some care: we need to treat low and high regularity separately in several steps.

Substep
2.1.
Well-posedness of energy solutions for (
3.13
).

On the one hand, using the incompressibility constraint, the energy identity for the transport-diffusion equation for
\rho_{0}
reads

(3.14)

\|{\rho}_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{2}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla{\rho}_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}=\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}.

On the other hand, for
\rho_{0}\geq 0
, the energy identity for
{u}_{0}
takes the form

\displaystyle\|\nabla{u}_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,\leq\,2\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(1+c_{0}\rho_{0})|\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})|^{2}\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}hu_{0},

and thus, using
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{0}=0
,

(3.15)

\|\nabla{u}_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\leq\,\|h\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

By a standard fixed-point approach,
in view of these a priori estimates,
given an initial condition
\rho^{\circ}\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
\rho^{\circ}\geq 0
and given
h\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, we easily check that the system (
3.13
) is globally well-posed with
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
and
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
with
\rho_{0}\geq 0
; we skip the detail.

Substep
2.2.
H^{1}
regularity for
(\nabla u_{0},\rho_{0})
: provided that the initial condition further satisfies
\rho^{\circ}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and provided that
h\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, we show that
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and that
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{2})
, with

(3.16)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\leq\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ}),

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})&:&\text{if $d=2$},\\
\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})&:&\text{if $d=3$},\end{array}\right.

where henceforth
C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})
stands for a constant further depending on an upper bound on
t,c_{0}
, and on the controlled norms of the data
h
and
\rho^{\circ}
.

We start with the proof of (
3.16
).
Testing the equation for
\rho_{0}
with
\Delta\rho_{0}
, and using the incompressibility of
u_{0}
, we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(u_{0}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0})\Delta\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{0}:(\nabla\rho_{0}\otimes\nabla\rho_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}^{2}.

In dimension
d<4
, we can appeal to the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality

\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{1-\frac{d}{4}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{4}}.

The above then becomes, further using the elliptic estimate
\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lesssim\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}
,

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2(1-\frac{d}{4})}\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{2}},

where the last factor can now be absorbed in the left-hand side by Young’s inequality, to the effect of

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

By Grönwall’s inequality and the a priori energy estimate (
3.15
), this precisely proves the claim (
3.16
).

Next, we turn to the proof of the corresponding estimate (
3.2
) for
u_{0}
. Testing the equation for
u_{0}
with
\Delta u_{0}
, we find

\displaystyle\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}h\cdot\Delta u_{0}-2c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}{\operatorname{div}}(\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}))\cdot\Delta u_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}h\cdot\Delta u_{0}-c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{0}|\Delta u_{0}|^{2}-2c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\nabla\rho_{0})\cdot\Delta u_{0},

and thus, by Young’s inequality,

\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+c_{0}\|\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

In dimension
d<4
, appealing to the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality, we can estimate for all
2<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
and
d\leq p,q\leq\frac{2d}{d-2}
,

\displaystyle\|\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{p}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,q}

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{1-\frac{d}{q}}\|\nabla^{2}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{q}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{1-\frac{d}{p}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{p}}.

Inserting the a priori estimates (
3.15
) and (
3.16
), combining with the above, and using Young’s inequality to absorb the
H^{2}
norm of
u_{0}
, we are led to

\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{p,q}\,\|h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+C(t,h)\|\nabla\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{q}{q-d}-\frac{d}{2}\frac{q-2}{q-d}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{2}\frac{q-2}{q-d}}.

Choosing any
q<\infty
if
d=2
, and choosing
q=\frac{2d}{d-2}
if
d=3
, we get

(3.19)

\|\Delta u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{\gamma}\,\|h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+C(t,h)\|\nabla\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\gamma}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{2}{4-d}},

where we can take any exponent
\gamma>0
if
d=2
, and
\gamma=0
if
d=3
.
Combined with (
3.16
), this yields the claim (
3.2
).

Substep
2.3.
H^{2}
regularity for
(\nabla u_{0},\rho_{0})
: provided that the initial condition further satisfies
\rho^{\circ}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and provided that
h\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, we show that
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and that
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})\cap\operatorname{L}^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, with

(3.20)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla^{2}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,\leq\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ}),

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\|\nabla^{3}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\leq\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})&:&\text{if $d=2$},\\
\|\nabla^{3}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\leq\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})&:&\text{if $d=3$}.\end{array}\right.

We start with the proof of (
3.20
).
Applying
\nabla^{2}
to both sides of the equation for
\rho_{0}
, testing with
\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}
, and integrating by parts, we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\nabla^{2}_{ij}\rho_{0})\nabla^{2}_{ij}\big((u_{0})_{k}\nabla_{k}\rho_{0}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\nabla^{3}\rho_{0})_{ijk}(\nabla u_{0})_{ki}(\nabla\rho_{0})_{j}+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\nabla^{3}\rho_{0})_{ijj}(\nabla u_{0})_{ki}(\nabla\rho_{0})_{k},

and thus, using Young’s inequality to absorb the factors
\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}
in the right-hand side,

\partial_{t}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|\nabla u_{0}\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

By the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality, we can estimate for all
2<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
and
d\leq p,q\leq\frac{2d}{d-2}
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{p}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{1-\frac{d}{q}}\|\nabla^{2}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{q}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{1-\frac{d}{p}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{p}}.

Inserting the a priori estimates (
3.15
), (
3.16
), and (
3.19
), and combining with the above, we are led to

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\leq\,C(t,h)\|\nabla\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2(1-\frac{d}{p})}\Big(\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{2d}{p}}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{2d}{p}+\frac{4d}{q(4-d)}}\Big).

Choosing any
q<\infty
if
d=2
, and choosing
q=\frac{2d}{d-2}
if
d=3
, this proves

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\leq\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+1&:&\text{if $d=2$,}\\
\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}&:&\text{if $d=3$.}\end{array}\right.

By Grönwall’s inequality, this yields

\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\\
\,\leq\,C(t,h)\|\nabla^{2}\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&:&\text{if $d=2$,}\\
\exp\big(C(t,h)\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\big)&:&\text{if $d=3$.}\end{array}\right.

By the a priori estimate (
3.16
), this proves the claimed estimate on
\rho_{0}
. Combined with (
3.19
), this concludes the proof of (
3.20
).

We turn to the proof of (
3.2
).
Applying
\nabla^{2}
to both sides of the equation for
u_{0}
, testing with
\nabla^{2}u_{0}
, integrating by parts, and using Young’s inequality, we find

\|\nabla^{3}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|\nabla h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla\rho_{0}\otimes\nabla^{2}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\otimes\nabla u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

and thus, using as above the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality and the a priori estimates (
3.15
), (
3.16
), and (
3.20
), we obtain

(3.23)

\|\nabla^{3}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,C(t,h,\rho^{\circ})\Big(1+\|\nabla^{3}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{d-2}\Big).

Combined with (
3.20
), this proves (
3.2
).

Substep
2.4.
H^{s}
regularity for
(\nabla u_{0},\rho_{0})
: for all integers
s>2
, provided that the initial condition further satisfies
\rho^{\circ}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and provided that
h\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, we show that
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and that
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
.
More precisely, we shall prove for all integers
s>2
,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\exp\big(C_{s}\|u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}\big),

(3.25)

\displaystyle\|u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|h\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}H^{s-1}_{x}}+\|u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}}\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}},

and we note that these estimates indeed yield the conclusion by a direct iteration, starting from the results
of Substep 2.3 for
s=3
.

Let
s>2
be a fixed integer, which entails in particular
s>\frac{d}{2}+1
as we consider dimension
d<4
.
We start with the proof of (
3.24
).
Applying
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}:=(1-\Delta_{x})^{s/2}
to both sides of the equation for
\rho_{0}
, and testing it with
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}
, we find

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0})\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}(u_{0}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}),

and thus, using the incompressibility constraint,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0})[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u_{0}\cdot\nabla]\rho_{0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u_{0}\cdot\nabla]\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

To estimate the last factor, we appeal to the following form of the Kato–Ponce commutator estimate
[
75
, Lemma X1]
(see also
[
85
, Lemma 3.4]
): for all
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
and
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we have

\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u\cdot\nabla]\rho\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|u\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|\rho\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{x}}+\|u\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{x}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}_{x}},

and thus, by the Sobolev embedding with
s>\frac{d}{2}+1
,

(3.27)

\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u\cdot\nabla]\rho\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|u\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}_{x}}.

Using this to estimate the last factor in (
3.26
), we are led to

(3.28)

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}_{x}},

and the claim (
3.24
) follows by Grönwall’s inequality.

We turn to the proof of (
3.25
). Applying
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}
to both sides of the equation for
u_{0}
, and testing it with
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}u_{0}
, we find

\displaystyle\|\nabla\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}u_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}h\cdot\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}u_{0}-2c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}):\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}(\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}h\cdot\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}u_{0}-2c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}):[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},\rho_{0}]\operatorname{D}(u_{0}),

and thus, by Young’s inequality,

\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},\rho_{0}]\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

To estimate the last factor, we now appeal to the following form of the Kato–Ponce commutator estimate, instead of (
3.27
),
with
s>\frac{d}{2}+1
,

\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},\rho]\operatorname{D}(u)\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|u\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}_{x}}.

Using this to estimate the last factor in the above, the claim (
3.25
) follows.

Substep
2.5. Time regularity.

For all
s\geq 0
, if
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, then the equation for
\rho_{0}
yields, by the Sobolev embedding in dimension
d<4
,

\partial_{t}\rho_{0}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta\rho_{0}-u_{0}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}\,\in\,\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})).

Next, taking the time-derivative of both sides of the equation for
u_{0}
, we find

-{\operatorname{div}}\big((1+c_{0}\rho_{0})2\!\operatorname{D}(\partial_{t}u_{0})\big)+\nabla\partial_{t}p_{0}=c_{0}{\operatorname{div}}\big((\partial_{t}\rho_{0})2\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})\big)+\partial_{t}h.

For all
s\geq 0
, if
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, we deduce by elliptic regularity that
\partial_{t}u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
provided
h\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
.
Higher time regularity is obtained similarly by direct induction.

Step
3. Proof of item (ii).

In the Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
, the system (
2.4
) reads

(3.29)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta u_{1}+\nabla p_{1}\,=\,{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[g_{2}])+{\operatorname{div}}\big(\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},\nabla u_{1}]+\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1},\nabla u_{0}]\big),\\[2.84526pt]
(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x}+u_{0}\cdot\nabla_{x})\rho_{1}+u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}\rho_{0}+\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{1}\rangle=0,\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u_{1})=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{1}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\rho_{1}|_{t=0}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
g_{1},g_{2}
are given by (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), respectively, in view of the explicit computations in Step 1.
Note that the form of
g_{1},g_{2}
ensures that this is a linear system for
(u_{1},\rho_{1})
.
We therefore focus on the proof of a priori energy estimates, while well-posedness and regularity properties easily follow.
On the one hand, testing the equation for
\rho_{1}
with
\rho_{1}
itself, and using the incompressibility constraint, we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{1}u_{1}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\rho_{1}U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{1}(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\Big(\|u_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}+|U_{0}|\|\nabla_{x}g_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\Big),

and thus, by the explicit formula (
3.1
) for
g_{1}
and by the Sobolev embedding,

(3.30)

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big(1+\|u_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big)\big(1+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{3}_{x}}+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}\big)^{2}.

On the other hand, testing the equation for
u_{1}
with
u_{1}
itself, we find

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla u_{1}|^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{1}:\sigma_{1}[g_{2}]-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{1}:\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},\nabla u_{1}]-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{1}:\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1},\nabla u_{0}],

and thus, recalling the definition of the elastic and viscous stresses, inserting the explicit formulas (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) for
g_{1},g_{2}
, collecting all quadratic terms in
\nabla u_{1}
in the left-hand side, and noting that integrals of monomials of odd degree on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
vanish and using again the incompressibility constraint,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla u_{1}|^{2}+\lambda(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\theta)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n:\nabla u_{1})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)\rho_{0}\\
\hskip-85.35826pt\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2d}\lambda\theta\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n:\nabla u_{1})(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id})\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)\\
\hskip 85.35826pt:\Big(\tfrac{1}{4}A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})-\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2}-d\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}^{2}\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\Big)\\
-\tfrac{1}{8}\lambda\theta\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n:\nabla u_{1})(n\otimes n:\nabla u_{0})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}n\bigg)\rho_{0}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{1}:\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1},\nabla u_{0}].

Noting that the second left-hand side term is nonnegative as
\rho_{0}\geq 0
, we deduce by the Sobolev embedding,

\|\nabla u_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|\rho_{1}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|u_{0}\|_{H^{3}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|u_{0}\|_{H^{3}_{x}}\\
+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}\|\partial_{t}u_{0}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}\big(1+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{3}_{x}}\big)^{2}.

Combining this with (
3.30
) and with the Poincaré and Grönwall inequalities, we deduce that
u_{1}
is controlled in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
and that
\rho_{1}
is controlled in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
provided that
u_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
and
\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
.
∎

3.3.
Proof of Proposition
3.2

We aim to estimate the remainder terms
u_{k,\varepsilon},f_{k,\varepsilon},\rho_{k,\varepsilon},g_{k,\varepsilon}
in the
\varepsilon
-expansion of the solution
(u_{\varepsilon},f_{\varepsilon})
of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley system, as defined through

(3.31)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\varepsilon^{j}u_{j}+\varepsilon^{k}u_{k,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\varepsilon^{j}\rho_{j}+\varepsilon^{k}\rho_{k,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\varepsilon^{j}g_{j}+\varepsilon^{k}g_{k,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle f_{k,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{k,\varepsilon}+g_{k,\varepsilon},

for
k\leq 3
.
We split the proof into five steps. We shall constantly use the short-hand notation
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(t)
for multiplicative constants as defined in the statement,
the value of which may change from line to line.

Step
1. Energy estimate on the remainder
u_{3,\varepsilon}
.

Comparing equations for
u_{\varepsilon},u_{0},u_{1}
, cf. (
1.3
) and Proposition
3.1
, using the linearity of
\sigma_{1}
and the bilinearity of
\sigma_{2}
,
the remainder term
u_{3,\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-3}(u_{\varepsilon}-u_{0}-\varepsilon u_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}u_{2})
as defined in (
3.31
) satisfies the linearized Navier–Stokes equation

\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)u_{3,\varepsilon}-\Delta u_{3,\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f_{\varepsilon},\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}])+\nabla p_{3,\varepsilon}\\
=-\operatorname{Re}\big[(u_{3,\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)(u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2})+(u_{2}\cdot\nabla)u_{1}+((u_{1}+\varepsilon u_{2})\cdot\nabla)u_{2}\big]\\
+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[g_{4,\varepsilon}])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f_{3,\varepsilon},\nabla(u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2})])\\
+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[\rho_{2}+g_{2},\nabla u_{1}])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1}+\varepsilon(\rho_{2}+g_{2}),\nabla u_{2}]).

Here, we have also used the fact that
\sigma_{1}[\tau]=0
for any function
\tau
depending only on
x
, which follows from the simple observation that
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id})\,\mathrm{d}n=0
.
Testing this equation with
u_{3,\varepsilon}
, using the incompressibility constraints, and inserting the definition of elastic and viscous stresses
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
, cf. (
1.3
), we get the following energy identity,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\lambda\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(n\otimes n:\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\big)^{2}f_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{3,\varepsilon}\otimes u_{3,\varepsilon}:\nabla(u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2})

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{3,\varepsilon}\cdot\big((u_{2}\cdot\nabla)u_{1}+((u_{1}+\varepsilon u_{2})\cdot\nabla)u_{2}\big)

\displaystyle-\lambda\theta\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}:\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id})\,g_{4,\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}:\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)(\nabla(u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}))(n\otimes n)f_{3,\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}:\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)(\nabla u_{1})(n\otimes n)(\rho_{2}+g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}:\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)(\nabla u_{2})(n\otimes n)(\rho_{1}+g_{1}+\varepsilon(\rho_{2}+g_{2}))\,\mathrm{d}n\Big),

where we note that the viscous stress
\sigma_{2}[f_{\varepsilon},\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}]
has led to an additional dissipation term in the left-hand side.
Appealing to the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and to Young’s inequality, we may then deduce

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\quad\operatorname{Re}\left(1+\|(\nabla u_{0},\nabla u_{1},\nabla u_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}\right)\Big(\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|(u_{1},u_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\Big)+\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda^{2}\|(\nabla u_{0},\nabla u_{1},\nabla u_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}\|(f_{3,\varepsilon},g_{1},g_{2},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

Recalling the short-hand notation
\mathcal{C}
for multiplicative constants, and further decomposing
f_{3,\varepsilon}=\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3}+\varepsilon g_{4,\varepsilon}
, we get

(3.32)

\operatorname{Re}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\\
\,\leq\,\mathcal{C}+\mathcal{C}\Big(\operatorname{Re}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\Big)+(\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}+\mathcal{C}\varepsilon^{2})\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

Step
2. Energy estimate for
\rho_{3,\varepsilon}
.

The equation satisfied by
\rho_{3,\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-3}(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{0}-\varepsilon\rho_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}\rho_{2})
takes the form

\partial_{t}\rho_{3,\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x}\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\,=\,-u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}-u_{3,\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla(\rho_{0}+\varepsilon\rho_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\rho_{2})\\
-u_{2}\cdot\nabla\rho_{1}-(u_{1}+\varepsilon u_{2})\cdot\nabla\rho_{2}+\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{3,\varepsilon}\rangle.

Testing it with
\rho_{3,\varepsilon}
itself, we can use the incompressibility constraint and several integrations by parts to get

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\Big(\|(\rho_{0},\varepsilon\rho_{1},\varepsilon^{2}\rho_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}\|(u_{1},u_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|g_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\Big)\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

hence, after absorption in the spatial dissipation,

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\big(\operatorname{Pe}\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|g_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\big).

Using the maximum principle

(3.33)

\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t,x}}\,\leq\,\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}},

which holds for a (regular) solution
\rho_{0}
of (
2.3
) with initial data
\rho^{\circ}
, we deduce

(3.34)

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\big(\operatorname{Pe}\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|g_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\big).

Step
3. Energy estimate for
g_{4,\varepsilon}
.

The equation satisfied by
g_{4,\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-4}(g_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon g_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}g_{2}-\varepsilon^{3}g_{3})

takes the form

(3.35)

\varepsilon\partial_{t}g_{4,\varepsilon}-\Delta_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x}g_{4,\varepsilon}\\
\,=\,\sum_{i=1}^{7}\mathrm{T}_{i},

in terms of

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x})g_{3}-{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big(u_{1}g_{2}+u_{2}(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})\big),

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-{\operatorname{div}}_{x}(u_{\varepsilon}g_{3,\varepsilon}),

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}_{x}(u_{3,\varepsilon}(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})),

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}_{x}P_{1}^{\perp}\big(U_{0}nf_{3,\varepsilon}\big),

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})nf_{3,\varepsilon}\big),

\displaystyle T_{6}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon})n(\rho_{0}+\varepsilon f_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}f_{2})\big),

\displaystyle T_{7}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{1})nf_{2}+\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{2})n(f_{1}+\varepsilon f_{2})\big).

Testing this equation with
g_{4,\varepsilon}
itself, we separately analyze the effect of the seven different source terms. For
{T}_{1},{T}_{6}
, and
{T}_{7}
, we integrate by parts, we use Poincaré’s inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, recalling
\langle g_{4,\varepsilon}\rangle=0
, and we use the maximum principle (
3.33
), to the effect of

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{1}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}},

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{6}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\big(\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}_{x}^{\infty}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\big)\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}},

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{7}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

For the term
{T}_{2}
, we use the incompressibility constraint, we further decompose
g_{3,\varepsilon}=g_{3}+\varepsilon g_{4,\varepsilon}
, and we integrate by parts, to the effect of

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{2}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}\,{\operatorname{div}}(u_{\varepsilon}g_{3,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{3}+\varepsilon\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{3},

which is then estimated by

\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{2}g_{4,\varepsilon}\,\leq\,\mathcal{C}\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}},

again using Poincaré’s inequality. For the term
{T}_{3}
, we get

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{3}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}u_{3,\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

For the term
{T}_{4}
, decomposing
f_{3,\varepsilon}=\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3}+\varepsilon g_{4,\varepsilon}
, using that
U_{0}
is a constant and that
P_{1}^{\bot}g_{4,\varepsilon}=g_{4,\varepsilon}
, and integrating by parts, we find

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{4}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\,=\,\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{x}P_{1}^{\perp}\big(U_{0}nf_{3,\varepsilon}\big)

\displaystyle\,=\,\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big(U_{0}n(\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3})\big)+\varepsilon U_{0}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{x}(ng_{4,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\,=\,\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big(U_{0}n(\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3})\big),

which is then estimated by

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{4}g_{4,\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}n\,\lesssim\,\big(\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\mathcal{C}\big)\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

Finally, decomposing
f_{3,\varepsilon}=\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3}+\varepsilon g_{4,\varepsilon}
and
u_{\varepsilon}=u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1,\varepsilon}
, and integrating by parts, we split the remaining term
{T}_{5}
as follows,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{5}g_{4,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})nf_{3,\varepsilon}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})n(\rho_{3,\varepsilon}+g_{3}+\varepsilon g_{4,\varepsilon})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\rho_{3,\varepsilon}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})n\big)+\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})ng_{3}\big)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|g_{4,\varepsilon}|^{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\rho_{3,\varepsilon}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{0})n\big)+\varepsilon\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\rho_{3,\varepsilon}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon})n\big)

\displaystyle+\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g_{4,\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{\varepsilon})ng_{3}\big)+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|g_{4,\varepsilon}|^{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{0})n)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|g_{4,\varepsilon}|^{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon})n),

which is then estimated by

\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}{T}_{5}g_{4,\varepsilon}\,\lesssim\,\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big)\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\\
+\varepsilon\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big(\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}\big).

Testing the equation (
3.35
) with
g_{4,\varepsilon}
itself and using the above estimates on the different source terms, together with Young’s inequality, we end up with

(3.36)

\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\operatorname{Pe}}\|\nabla_{x}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\big)+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\big(\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}\\
+\varepsilon\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}^{2}+\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}\big)+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

In order to estimate the
\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}
norms in the right-hand side, we appeal to interpolation and to the Sobolev inequality on the torus in the following form: in dimension
d\leq 3
, we have for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}\quad

\displaystyle\leq\quad C\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2(1-\frac{d}{4})}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\quad\delta\|\nabla_{x}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+C\delta^{-\frac{d}{4-d}}\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

Likewise, further noting that
\rho_{3,\varepsilon}
is mean-free (given that the defining equations for
\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
ensure
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{2}=0
and
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho_{0}=1
), we have for all
\eta>0
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2(1-\frac{d}{4})}\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+C\eta^{-\frac{d}{4-d}}\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

Inserting these bounds into (
3.36
), and choosing
\delta\simeq\operatorname{Pe}^{-1}
and
\eta\simeq 1
, we get for
\varepsilon\lesssim 1

(3.37)

\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\big)+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\big(\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}\\
+\varepsilon\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\Big(1+\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big)\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

Step
4. Conclusion.

Combining estimates (
3.34
) and (
3.37
) in such a way that the term
\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}
in the right-hand side of (
3.37
) can be absorbed into the left-hand side of (
3.34
), we obtain

\operatorname{Pe}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\big)+\big((1+\operatorname{Pe})^{2}\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|g_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\big)\\
+\varepsilon\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\Big(1+\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big)\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

Further using the fact that

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}+\varepsilon^{6}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2},\qquad\|g_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\|\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}+\varepsilon^{2}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2},

the above reduces to

(3.38)

\operatorname{Pe}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}\big(1+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\big)+\big((1+\operatorname{Pe})^{2}\|\rho_{0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}\\
+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\Big(1+\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big)\Big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big).

We now aim to combine this with (
3.32
) to conclude the proof, and we separately consider the Stokes and Navier–Stokes cases, splitting the proof into two further substeps.

Substep
4.1. Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
,
d\leq 3
.

In the Stokes case, the estimate (
3.32
) becomes, using Poincaré’s inequality,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}+\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}+\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\mathcal{C}\big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\big).

We now insert this estimate for
u_{3,\varepsilon}
in the right-hand side of (
3.38
).
Provided that

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}(1+\operatorname{Pe})^{2}\big(\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\,\ll\,1

is small enough, we can absorb the term involving
\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}
in the estimate for
u_{3,\varepsilon}
, and we end up with

\operatorname{Pe}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}+\Big(\mathcal{C}+\varepsilon\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big)\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\Big(1+\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big)\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

By Grönwall’s inequality, we deduce

\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t,x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\leq\,\mathcal{C}(t)\Big(1+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big)\exp\bigg(\mathcal{C}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\bigg).

Now expanding
u_{1,\varepsilon}=u_{1}+\varepsilon u_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{3,\varepsilon}

and using once more (
3.39
), we get

\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t,x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\leq\,\mathcal{C}(t)\Big(1+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big)\exp\bigg(\varepsilon^{4}\mathcal{C}(t)\Big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\Big)^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\bigg).

The well-preparedness assumption (
3.3
) precisely ensures that the initial terms in the right-hand side are uniformly bounded.
Using again Poincaré’s inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and appealing to a standard continuity argument, the stated estimates on
\rho_{3,\varepsilon}
and
g_{4,\varepsilon}
follow. The stated estimate on
u_{3,\varepsilon}
is then deduced from (
3.39
).

Substep
4.2. Navier–Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
,
d=2
.

In the Navier–Stokes case,
provided that

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\theta^{2}(1+\operatorname{Pe})^{2}\big(\|\rho^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{C}\big)\,\ll\,1

is small enough,
combining (
3.32
) and (
3.38
) in such a way that the term
\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}
in the right-hand side of (
3.38
) can be absorbed into the corresponding dissipation term in (
3.32
), while further absorbing the term
\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}
in the right-hand side of (
3.32
) into the corresponding dissipation term in (
3.38
),
we obtain

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\operatorname{Pe}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}\Big(1+\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\Big)+\varepsilon\mathcal{C}\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big(\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big),

and thus, by Grönwall’s inequality,

\|u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla u_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{3,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}g_{4,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t,x,n}}^{2}\\
\,\lesssim\,\mathcal{C}(t)\Big(1+\|u_{3,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\rho_{3,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|g_{4,\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big)\exp\Big(\mathcal{C}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u_{1,\varepsilon}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{4}{4-d}}\Big).

In the 2D case, the exponent in the exponential reduces to
\frac{4}{4-d}=2
. Then expanding again
u_{1,\varepsilon}=u_{1}+\varepsilon u_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{3,\varepsilon}
, we can appeal to a continuity argument and the conclusion follows by noting that the well-preparedness assumption (
3.3
) precisely ensure the uniform boundedness of the initial terms.∎

3.4.
Proof of Proposition
3.3

The proof mainly consists in inserting the explicit expressions for
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
computed in Proposition
3.1
in terms of
(u_{0},\rho_{0})
and
(u_{1},\rho_{1})
inside the systems (
2.3
) and (
2.4
).
Recalling the definition of stress tensors in (
1.3
), inserting the explicit expressions for
g_{1},g_{2}
in Proposition
3.1
, and using the elementary integral identities in (
3.11
), we are led to

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}[g_{1}]\,=\,\lambda\theta\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id})\,g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}n\,=\,\lambda\theta\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}),

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},u_{0}]\,=\,\lambda\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)\nabla u_{0}(n\otimes n)\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}n\,=\,\lambda\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0}).

This proves that the couple
(u_{0},\rho_{0})
solves the system (
2.6
) with parameters
\eta_{0}=1
and
\eta_{1}=\lambda\tfrac{\omega_{d}(\theta+2)}{2d(d+2)}
. Now turning to
(u_{1},\rho_{1})
, we compute

\sigma_{1}[g_{2}]\,=\,\lambda\theta\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}n\\
\,=\,\lambda\theta\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\big(\rho_{1}\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})+\rho_{0}\!\operatorname{D}(u_{1})\big)+\lambda\theta\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\bigg(\!-\tfrac{1}{4d}A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})+2\tfrac{d+2}{d(d+4)}\rho_{0}\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2}\\
+\tfrac{1}{d(d-1)}U_{0}^{2}\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}-\tfrac{1}{d(d+4)}{\operatorname{tr}}(\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2})\operatorname{Id}\bigg).

We also compute

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}[\rho_{0},\nabla u_{1}]\,=\,\lambda\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)(\nabla u_{1})(n\otimes n)\,\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}n\,=\,\lambda\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\rho_{0}\!\operatorname{D}(u_{1}),

as well as

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1},\nabla u_{0}]\,=\,\lambda\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}\rho_{1}\!\operatorname{D}(u_{0})+\lambda\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)(d+4)}\rho_{0}\Big(2\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{tr}}(\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2})\operatorname{Id}\Big).

Inserting these identities into (
2.4
), we verify that
u_{1}
satisfies the fluid equation in (
2.7
) for some modified pressure field
p_{1}
and with the expected coefficients

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}=-\lambda\theta\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{4d^{2}(d+2)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma_{2}=\lambda\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{2d^{2}(d+4)}\big(\theta+\tfrac{2d}{d+2}\big).

We turn to the derivation of the corresponding equation for
\rho_{1}
. By the defining equations for
\rho_{1}
in (
2.4
), we can write

(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u_{0}\cdot\nabla)\rho_{1}=-u_{1}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}-\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{1}\rangle.\\

Inserting the explicit expressions for
g_{1}
in Proposition
3.1
, and using the above computations, we find

\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{1}\rangle\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{d-1}U_{0}^{2}\langle n\otimes n:\nabla^{2}\rho_{0}\rangle\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{d(d-1)}U_{0}^{2}\Delta\rho_{0},

and the conclusion follows for
\rho_{1}
.
∎

4.
Modelling and physical aspects of the results

In this section, we review the different models considered in this work and their physical content. We start with the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model (
1.3
) for suspensions and we describe in particular the signification of the different parameters. Next, we turn to ordered fluid models: we recall their standard definition and the non-Newtonian properties that they describe, before turning to non-standard modifications of those models at finite Péclet number and for inhomogeneous suspensions, leading to the specific equations (
1.1
) that we have derived in the hydrodynamic approximation. Finally, we compare the explicit non-Newtonian properties of the latter qualitatively with experimental data, and we discuss how predictions get modified in the next-order (third-order fluid) description.

In the present paper we deal with the rigorous derivation of macroscopic models from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model.

4.1.
Doi and Doi–Saintillan–Shelley models

As outlined at the beginning of the introduction, the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model is a
multiscale
kinetic model for suspensions, which can be viewed as intermediate between
microscopic
fluid-particle models and non-Newtonian
macroscopic
fluid models. In microscopic models, particles are modelled as rigid bodies in the fluid evolving according to individual ODEs or SDEs coupled to a fluid PDE. In contrast, in so-called
multiscale
models, particles are only described via their density, which satisfies some kinetic equation and affects the fluid flow in some simplified way: in a nutshell, it essentially amounts to neglecting multi-body effects.
The usual dimensional form of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model reads (see
[
27
, Chapter 8]
and
[
99
]
)

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\rho_{\operatorname{fl}}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}\Delta u+\nabla p\,=\,h+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[f])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla u]),\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}f+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((u+V_{0}n)f\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)nf\big)\\
\hskip 142.26378pt=D_{\operatorname{tr}}{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((\operatorname{Id}+n\otimes n)\nabla_{x}f\big)+D_{\operatorname{ro}}\Delta_{n}f,\\[5.69054pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\end{array}\right.

where elastic and viscous stresses are respectively given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}[f]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(3k_{B}\Theta+\alpha\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}|V_{0}|\ell^{2}\right)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla u]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n):\operatorname{D}(u)(n\otimes n)\,f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n,

where
h
is an internal force,
and where the different parameters
\rho_{\operatorname{fl}},\mu_{\operatorname{fl}},D_{\operatorname{tr}},D_{\operatorname{ro}},\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}},V_{0},\ell
are all assumed to be constant.
In this kinetic description,
\rho_{\operatorname{fl}}
stands for the fluid density,
\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}
for the solvent viscosity,
D_{\operatorname{tr}}
and
D_{\operatorname{ro}}
for the translational and rotational diffusion coefficients,
\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}
for the rotational resistance coefficient,
V_{0}
for the self-propulsion speed of the particles, and
\ell
for the length of the particles.
The fluid flow is coupled to the kinetic equation for the particle density via the additional stresses
\sigma_{1}
(elastic stress) and
\sigma_{2}
(viscous stress), which make the fluid equations non-Newtonian.
We briefly describe the structure and physical origin of those contributions:

—

The viscous stress
\sigma_{2}
arises from the rigidity of suspended particles in the fluid flow: it is formally understood by homogenization of the solid phase, viewed as inclusions with infinite shear viscosity in the fluid.
The above expression (
4.2
) is an approximation for very elongated particles: for general axisymmetric particles, the viscous stress involves additional terms depending on the precise shape of the particles (see e.g.
[
64
]
for spheroids), but slender body theory indeed shows that it reduces to the above form in the limit of very elongated particles (see e.g.
[
76
, Section 3.4]
).

—

The elastic stress
\sigma_{1}
contains a passive and an active contribution. The passive part, proportional to the the Boltzmann constant
k_{B}
and to the absolute temperature
\Theta
, is created by the random torques that are responsible for the rotational Brownian motion of the particles.
These torques indeed create stresses due to the rigidity and anisotropy of the particles.
On the other hand, the active part arises directly from the swimming mechanism, which is encoded in the parameter
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
: a so-called puller particle corresponds to
\alpha>0
, and a pusher particle corresponds to
\alpha<0
.

For
V_{0}=0
, the model (
4.1
) reduces to the classical Doi model for passive Brownian particles
[
27
]
.
Finally, for very elongated particles in 3D, we also note that the translational and rotational diffusion and resistance coefficients are asymptotically given as follows (see
[
26
, Section 5.15]
),

(4.3)

\begin{array}[]{rllrrll}D_{\operatorname{ro}}&=&\tfrac{k_{B}\Theta}{\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}}+D_{\operatorname{act}},&&\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}&=&\tfrac{\pi\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}\ell^{3}}{3\log(\ell/a)},\\[5.69054pt]
D_{\operatorname{tr}}&=&\tfrac{k_{B}\Theta}{\zeta_{\operatorname{tr}}},&&\zeta_{\operatorname{tr}}&=&\tfrac{2\pi\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}\ell}{\log(\ell/a)},\end{array}

where
a
is the width of the particles and where
D_{\operatorname{act}}
is some possible active contribution to the rotational diffusion (tumbling). We briefly explain how our starting point (
1.3
) in this work follows from the above usual form (
4.1
) of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model up to some simplification and non-dimensionalization procedure, and we then briefly recall the history of such Doi-type models.

4.1.1.
Simplification and non-dimensionalization

Compared to (
4.1
), we make two minor simplifications in the model:

—

While the translational diffusion in (
4.1
) is proportional to
\operatorname{Id}+n\otimes n
, hence is twice as strong in the direction of particle orientation as in the orthogonal directions, we choose to neglect this
O(1)
difference and assume that the diffusion is isotropic. This choice is for simplicity and does not change anything in the analysis of the model.

—

It has been observed in the seminal work
[
4
]
that, for the example of E. coli bacteria and related microswimmers, the contribution of thermal rotation and active tumbling is of the same order, and we therefore set
D_{\operatorname{act}}=0
for simplicity.

After these two simplifications, we non-dimensionalize the model (
4.1
) in terms of the typical speed
u_{0}
of the fluid, the typical macroscopic length scale
L
, and the typical number
N
of particles in a cube of side length
L
. We further rescale time according to the timescale of the fluid flow, that is,
T=L/u_{0}
. More precisely, we define

\displaystyle\hat{u}(t,x):=\tfrac{1}{u_{0}}u(Tt,Lx),

\displaystyle\hat{f}(t,x):=\tfrac{L^{d}}{N}f(Tt,Lx),

\displaystyle\hat{h}(t,x):=\tfrac{L^{2}}{\mu u_{0}}h(Tt,Lx).

This leads to the following dimensionless model, dropping the hats for simplicity,

(4.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-\Delta u+\nabla p\,=\,h+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[f])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla u]),\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}f+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((u+U_{0}n)f\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)nf\big)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x}f+\frac{1}{\operatorname{Wi}}\Delta_{n}f,\\[5.69054pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\end{array}\right.

where the dimensionless counterparts of the additional stresses
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
now take the form

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}[f]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(6\tfrac{\lambda}{\operatorname{Wi}}+\gamma)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla u]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(n\otimes n)(\nabla u)(n\otimes n)\,f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n.

Here,
\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pe},\operatorname{Wi}>0
stand for the so-called Reynolds, Péclet, and Weissenberg numbers,
\lambda>0
depends only on the shape and number density of the particles, and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
accounts for the active contribution to the stress.
More precisely, these parameters are given by

\begin{array}[]{rllrrll}\operatorname{Re}&=&\tfrac{\rho_{\operatorname{fl}}u_{0}L}{\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}},&&\lambda&=&\tfrac{\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}N}{2\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}L^{d}},\\[5.69054pt]
\operatorname{Pe}&=&\tfrac{u_{0}L\zeta_{\operatorname{tr}}}{k_{B}\Theta},&&\operatorname{Wi}&=&\tfrac{u_{0}\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}}{k_{B}\Theta L},\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma&=&\alpha N\tfrac{|U_{0}|\ell^{2}}{L^{d-1}},&&U_{0}&=&\tfrac{V_{0}}{u_{0}},\end{array}

and we briefly comment on their range and interpretation:

—

The Weissenberg number
\operatorname{Wi}
is the ratio between convection and relaxation timescales. For the kinetic viscoelastic models under consideration, it coincides with the rotational Péclet number. For elongated particles in 3D, as
\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}
is proportional to the cube of the particle length, cf. (
4.3
), the regime when
\operatorname{Wi}
is of order
O(1)
is very narrow, and lies under standard flow conditions at particle lengths of around
10
microns. In this work, we are interested in the derivation of hydrodynamic approximations in case of very small
\operatorname{Wi}\ll 1
: this means that we have applications in mind where the particles have a length of a few microns and below, which is in particular the case for many types of bacteria. For notational convenience, we rename the Weissenberg number as

\varepsilon\,:=\,\operatorname{Wi}\ll 1.

—

The (translational) Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}
is typically much larger than its rotational counterpart
\operatorname{Wi}

since
1
1

1

The same holds with an additional logarithmic correction in 2D.

\tfrac{\operatorname{Pe}}{\operatorname{Wi}}\,=\,\tfrac{\zeta_{\operatorname{tr}}L^{2}}{\zeta_{\operatorname{ro}}}\,=\,\tfrac{6L^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\,\gg\,1.

In fact, from the application-oriented perspective, it makes sense to consider
\operatorname{Pe}\gg 1
. However, due to well-posedness and stability issues, our analysis crucially relies on keeping
\operatorname{Pe}
not too large, and we shall generally assume
\operatorname{Pe}\simeq 1
. We could actually allow for
\operatorname{Pe}
to slightly diverge, but more slowly than the inverse of the volume fraction of the particles, cf. (
2.9
), which is anyway incompatible with typical applications.

—

The shape parameter
\lambda
is typically quite small as it is proportional to the volume fraction
NL^{-d}
of the particles.

—

The prefactor in the viscous stress
\sigma_{1}
reads

6\tfrac{\lambda}{\operatorname{Wi}}+\gamma\,=\,\tfrac{\lambda}{\operatorname{Wi}}\Big(6+2\alpha\tfrac{|V_{0}|\ell^{2}\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}{k_{B}\Theta}\Big),

where the term
\frac{|V_{0}|\ell^{2}\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}{k_{B}\Theta}
is of order
1-10
for typical microswimmers like E. coli bacteria
[
4
]
. We shall set for abbreviation

(4.5)

\displaystyle\theta\,:=\,6+2\alpha\tfrac{|V_{0}|\ell^{2}\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}{k_{B}\Theta}.

Note in particular that for passive particles
V_{0}=0
we find
\theta=6
.

—

The self-propulsion speed
V_{0}
of active particles is typically around
10
micron per second.
This is so slow with respect to typical shear flows considered in experimental settings that the ratio
U_{0}=\frac{V_{0}}{u_{0}}
is typically tiny.
However, extremely low shear rates leading to
u_{0}\sim V_{0}
can possibly also be achieved, as for instance in the experiments reported by
[
84
]
. For that reason, we choose not to neglect
U_{0}
in the equations and to keep track of its effects.

This non-dimensionalized model (
4.4
) coincides with (
1.3
) upon setting
\operatorname{Wi}=\varepsilon
and
\theta
as in (
4.5
), and it constitutes our starting point in this work.

4.1.2.
Derivation of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model

For completeness, we briefly review the history of the derivation of Doi-type models.
In the physics literature, the systematic theoretical study of the effective rheology of suspensions has been initiated by Einstein
[
38
]
, who found that passive non-Brownian spherical rigid particles effectively increase the fluid viscosity by
\frac{5}{2}\phi\eta
, where
\phi
is the volume fraction of the particles and
\eta
is the viscosity of the solvent. Jeffery
[
71
]
studied the analogous problem for ellipsoidal particles and found an increase of the viscosity depending locally on orientations of the particles. By slender-body theory, in the limit of very elongated particles, Jeffery’s viscous stress exactly takes the form of
\sigma_{2}
in (
1.3
); see e.g.
[
10
,
76
]
.
Starting in the 1930s, there is a vast literature in physics on the rheology of suspensions of non-spherical rigid particles, see e.g.
[
77
,
39
,
89
,
11
]
, but this early work was restricted to specific fluid flows like simple shear, and Brownian effects were neglected.
Brownian particles were first considered in
[
104
,
78
,
95
,
102
]
, by means of different models and justifications, finally leading to the additional elastic stress
\sigma_{1}
in (
1.3
).
These models were largely reviewed in
[
81
,
64
,
10
]
:
in particular, the multiscale kinetic model (
1.3
) in the passive case (
U_{0}=0
,
\theta=6
) then entered textbooks such as
[
27
,
53
]
and became known in the mathematical literature as the Doi model.
The extension to active suspensions has been proposed by Saintillan and Shelley
[
101
]
: by coarse-graining force dipoles exerted by the particles on the fluid, they derived a further contribution of the elastic stress
\sigma_{1}
due to particle activity; see also
[
55
,
57
,
97
,
92
,
25
]
.

All of the aforementioned derivations are of formal or phenomenological nature. On the mathematical side, the derivation of the viscous stress
\sigma_{2}
from microscopic models has received considerable attention in recent years. When the fluid is modeled by the Stokes equations and the particle distribution is given, the effective increase of the fluid viscosity is by now well understood
[
56
,
87
,
45
,
63
,
34
,
44
,
46
,
31
,
47
,
29
,
33
]
; see
[
32
]
for a review.
In a similar setting, for active suspensions, the elastic stress
\sigma_{1}
has been derived in
[
49
,
5
]
.
Regarding the derivation of the passive part of the elastic stress
\sigma_{1}
for Brownian particles, we refer
to
[
66
]
, where the authors start from a simplified microscopic model where the particle dynamics is given by Brownian motion and not coupled to the fluid.
Yet, non-Newtonian effects originate from the retroaction of the fluid on the particles, that is, from coupling the particle dynamics to the fluid flow: beyond the derivation of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
in steady regimes, the derivation of kinetic equations for the particle density is thus of key interest.
First steps in that direction
have been undertaken in
[
68
,
67
,
30
]
,
but we emphasize that a complete derivation is a very challenging open problem for which the existing rigorous results are still highly fragmentary.

4.2.
Ordered fluid models

Various non-Newtonian fluid models have been considered in the literature, taking into account nonlinear and memory effects in different ways on the macroscopic scale.
In this work, we focus on so-called ordered fluid models that are shown to naturally appear as hydrodynamic approximations of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory.
In this section, we start by recalling the usual form of such models, we review their non-Newtonian properties, we motivate their non-standard modification at finite Péclet number and for inhomogeneous suspensions, and summarize our findings regarding well-posedness and stability issues. Finally, we also discuss the explicit non-Newtonian parameters (
2.8
) that we have derived in the hydrodynamic approximation, and we comment on the physical content of the next-order approximation.

4.2.1.
Usual form of ordered fluid models

Ordered fluid equations take the form

(4.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma)+\nabla p=h,\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\end{array}\right.

where the stress
\sigma
is a function of the Rivlin–Ericksen tensors
\{A_{k}(u)\}_{k}
associated with the fluid velocity
u
, which are defined iteratively through

\displaystyle A_{1}(u)\,:=\,2\operatorname{D}(u)\,=\,\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{T},

(4.7)

\displaystyle A_{n+1}(u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)A_{n}(u)+(\nabla u)^{T}A_{n}(u)+A_{n}(u)\nabla u,\qquad n\geq 1.

In other words,
A_{n+1}(u)
is the so-called lower-convected derivative of
A_{n}(u)
, which ensures frame indifference of the equations.
2
2

2

In the literature,
upper
-convected instead of lower-convected derivatives are sometimes used to define ordered fluids (see e.g.
[
7
, Section 6]
). This is merely a choice of convention, as both lead to equivalent fluid equations (although the value and interpretation of parameters of course depends on the chosen convention). Indeed, the upper-convected derivative of
A_{n}(u)
can be rewritten in terms of the lower-convected derivative as

(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)A_{n}(u)-A_{n}(u)(\nabla u)^{T}-(\nabla u)A_{n}(u)\,=\,A_{n+1}(u)-\big(A_{1}(u)A_{n}(u)+A_{n}(u)A_{1}(u)\big).

While first-order fluids are of the form (
4.6
) with constitutive law
\sigma=\eta_{0}A_{1}(u)
, thus coinciding with standard Newtonian fluids,
second-order fluids are of the form (
4.6
) with

(4.8)

\displaystyle\sigma\,:=\,\eta_{0}A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}(u)+\alpha_{2}A_{1}(u)^{2},

see e.g.
[
9
, Eq. (8.48)]
, and third-order fluids amount to

(4.9)

\sigma\,:=\,\eta_{0}A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}(u)+\alpha_{2}A_{1}(u)^{2}\\
+\beta_{1}A_{3}(u)+\beta_{2}\big(A_{1}(u)A_{2}(u)+A_{2}(u)A_{1}(u)\big)+\beta_{3}A_{1}(u){\operatorname{tr}}\big(A_{1}(u)^{2}\big),

for some coefficients
\eta_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
.
3
3

3

In arbitrary space dimension
d
, a further term
\beta_{4}A_{1}(u)^{3}
should be included in general in the stress
\sigma
of third-order fluids, but it is redundant in dimensions
d\leq 3
as we then have
B^{3}=\frac{1}{2}B\,{\operatorname{tr}}(B^{2})+\frac{1}{3}{\operatorname{tr}}(B^{3})
by the Cayley–Hamilton theorem for any symmetric trace-free matrix
B
.

Since third- and higher-order fluid models are fairly complicated and involve a lot of terms, the most widely used ordered fluid model for viscoelastic fluids is the second-order model (
4.8
).
For more details on modelling aspects and applications, we refer to
[
36
,
9
,
6
,
72
,
91
]
.

4.2.2.
Non-Newtonian properties of ordered fluids

We recall the basic rheological properties of these ordered fluid models depending on the different parameter values; we focus here on the 3D setting.
The coefficient
\eta_{0}>0
in (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) is the zero-shear viscosity, as in the first-order (Newtonian) model, while other parameters account for various non-Newtonian behaviors as we briefly describe.

—

Shear-dependent viscosity:
In a simple shear flow
u_{0}(t,x)=\kappa x_{2}e_{1}
with shear rate
\kappa>0
, the shear-dependent viscosity is defined as

(4.10)

\eta(\kappa)\,:=\,\frac{\sigma_{12}}{\kappa}.

Noting that

\displaystyle\qquad A_{1}(u_{0})=\kappa\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{1}(u_{0})^{2}=\kappa^{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{2}(u_{0})=\kappa^{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&2&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

we compute for the second-order fluid that the shear-dependent viscosity simply coincides with the zero-shear viscosity,
\eta(\kappa)=\eta_{0}
. For the third-order model, in contrast, we find a nontrivial shear-dependent relation,

\eta(\kappa)=\eta_{0}+2\kappa^{2}(\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}).

Most real-life viscoelastic fluids, and in particular passive dilute suspensions, happen to be shear-thinning, meaning that the map
\kappa\mapsto\eta(\kappa)
is decreasing: this holds for the third-order fluid model provided that the coefficients satisfy
\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}<0
.

—

Normal-stress differences:

In a simple shear flow
u_{0}(t,x)=\kappa x_{2}e_{1}
with shear rate
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, non-Newtonian fluids typically display non-zero normal stresses. This is responsible for a number of phenomena, of which the rod-climbing effect is the best known; see e.g.
[
72
, Chapter 17]
and
[
6
, Chapter 6]
.
Normal-stress coefficients are defined as
4
4

4
