Beware of different sign conventions for the normal-stress coefficients. We follow here the choice of
[
9
, Chapter 2.2]
.

(4.11)

\displaystyle\nu_{10}\,:=\,\frac{\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{22}}{\kappa^{2}},

\displaystyle\nu_{20}\,:=\,\frac{\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{33}}{\kappa^{2}},

and thus, for second- and third-order models,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\nu_{10}\,=\,-2\alpha_{1},

\displaystyle\nu_{20}\,=\,2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}.

In other words,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are related to normal-stress coefficients via

(4.13)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\nu_{10},

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}\,=\,\nu_{10}+\nu_{20}.

For most real-life viscoelastic fluids, and in particular for polymer solutions, it is found experimentally that
\nu_{10}>0
,
\nu_{20}<0
, and that
|\nu_{20}|
is considerably smaller than
|\nu_{10}|
(by a factor of around
10
, see e.g.
[
9
, Section 2.2]
and
[
91
, Section 2.2]
),
which means in particular, in terms of second-order fluid coefficients,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}<0,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}>0.

—

Elongational viscosity:

The apparent viscosity of a non-Newtonian fluid may be completely different in an elongational flow. Given a uniaxial elongational flow
u_{0}(t,x)=\kappa(x_{1}e_{1}-\frac{1}{2}(x_{2}e_{2}+x_{3}e_{3}))
in the direction
e_{1}
, the elongational viscosity is defined as

(4.15)

\displaystyle\eta_{E}\,:=\,\frac{\sigma_{11}-\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{22}+\sigma_{33})}{\kappa}.

Noting that

(4.16)

\displaystyle A_{1}(u_{0})=\kappa\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{2}(u_{0})=A_{1}(u_{0})^{2}=\kappa^{2}\begin{pmatrix}4&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

we compute for the second-order model,

\displaystyle\eta_{E}\,=\,3\eta_{0}+3\kappa(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}).

For real-life viscoelastic fluids, it is typically observed that the elongational viscosity increases with the strain rate (so-called strain-thickening behavior), which holds provided that coefficients satisfy
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}>0
.

—

Retardation phase shift in oscillatory flow:

In a simple shear flow
u_{0}(t,x)=\kappa(t)x_{2}e_{1}
with oscillatory shear rate
\kappa(t)=\sin t
, we compute

A_{2}(u_{0})=\dot{\kappa}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}+\kappa^{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&2&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

which gives rise to a phase shift for
\sigma_{12}
in the second-order model, in form of

\sigma_{12}\,=\,\eta_{0}\kappa+\alpha_{1}\dot{\kappa}\,=\,(\eta_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sin\big(t+\arctan(\tfrac{\alpha_{1}}{\eta_{0}})\big).

This constitutes another typical (time-dependent) non-Newtonian feature, in link with the dependence of the stress on the flow history.

Remark 4.1

(Connection to Oldroyd–B model)
.

The Oldroyd–B model is a special case of a simple fluid model, which is particularly popular as a formal exact closure of the kinetic Hookean dumbbell model.
It is characterized by the following constitutive equation for the stress tensor,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\sigma=2\eta_{s}\operatorname{D}(u)+\eta_{p}\tau,

\displaystyle\tau+\operatorname{Wi}\left((\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\tau-\tau(\nabla u)^{T}-(\nabla u)\tau\right)\,=\,2\operatorname{D}(u).

By a formal expansion with respect to
\operatorname{Wi}\ll 1
, this model is found to agree to order
\operatorname{Wi}^{k}
with a
k
th-order fluid model with some specific choice of parameters. In particular, to order
\operatorname{Wi}^{2}
, we recover the second-order fluid model (
4.8
) with
\alpha_{2}=-2\alpha_{1}
, which means in particular that the second normal-stress coefficient vanishes,
\nu_{20}=0
.
We emphasize that this is
not
the case for the second-order fluid model that we derive from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory: the derived parameters in (
2.8
) yield in 3D

\tfrac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\,=\,\tfrac{\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}}\,=\,-\tfrac{10}{7}(1+\tfrac{6}{5\theta}),

which is indeed
\neq-2
provided
\theta\neq 3
. Recall that passive suspensions correspond to
\theta=6
. Hence, at small
\operatorname{Wi}
, the viscoelastic effects of suspensions of rigid Brownian particles are not well described by an Oldroyd–B model.
∎

4.2.3.
Modified ordered fluid models at
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty

While an infinite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
is usually considered in applications, we have needed to include a nontrivial spatial diffusion in the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model (
1.3
) in this work for technical stability reasons.
Now, at
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
, the definition of Rivlin–Ericksen tensors (
4.7
) then has to be adapted, leading to a natural modification of ordered fluid models for which we could surprisingly find no reference.
Note that this is reminiscent of the corresponding version of the Oldroyd–B model with stress diffusion that is often considered both for analytical and numerical studies
[
94
,
20
]
.
More precisely, Rivlin–Ericksen tensors are modified as follows,

(4.18)

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}(u)\,:=\,A_{1}(u)\,=\,2\operatorname{D}(u),

\displaystyle A_{n+1}^{\prime}(u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}-{\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)A_{n}^{\prime}(u)+(\nabla u)^{T}A_{n}^{\prime}(u)+A_{n}^{\prime}(u)(\nabla u),\quad n\geq 1,

hence in particular
A_{2}^{\prime}(u):=A_{2}(u)-\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta A_{1}(u)
.
The second-order fluid equations (
4.6
)–(
4.8
) are then replaced by

(4.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma)+\nabla p=h,\\[2.84526pt]
\sigma=\eta_{0}A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(u)+\alpha_{2}A_{1}(u)^{2},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0.\end{array}\right.

For higher-order fluid models, in this diffusive setting
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
, several additional tensors actually need to be further included in the stress beyond the above modified Rivlin–Ericksen tensors:
for the third-order model, instead of (
4.9
), the stress rather needs to be chosen in general as

(4.20)

\sigma\,=\,\eta_{0}A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(u)+\alpha_{2}A_{1}(u)^{2}+\beta_{1}A_{3}^{\prime}(u)+\beta_{1}^{\prime}B_{3}^{\prime}(u)\\
+\beta_{2}\big(A_{1}(u)A_{2}^{\prime}(u)+A_{2}^{\prime}(u)A_{1}(u)\big)+\beta_{3}A_{1}(u){\operatorname{tr}}\big(A_{1}(u)^{2}\big),

in terms of the following additional tensor,

B_{3}^{\prime}(u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)A_{1}(u)^{2}+\big((\nabla u)^{T}A_{1}(u)^{2}+A_{1}(u)^{2}(\nabla u)\big).

At
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
, this additional tensor is redundant as it indeed reduces to
B_{3}^{\prime}(u)=A_{1}(u)A_{2}(u)+A_{2}(u)A_{1}(u)-A_{1}(u)^{3}
, so we recover the usual form (
4.9
).
5
5

5

Recall that, as in (
4.9
), a further term
\beta_{4}A_{1}(u)^{3}
should always be included in the stress in arbitrary space dimension
d
, but it reduces to
\frac{1}{2}\beta_{4}A_{1}(u){\operatorname{tr}}(A_{1}(u)^{2})
in dimensions
d\leq 3
.

4.2.4.
Models for inhomogeneous suspensions

Whether at
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
or
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
, the above formulations of ordered fluid models describe the behavior of
spatially homogeneous
suspensions, that is, the behavior of fluids with a
uniform density
of suspended particles.
This can be naturally generalized to an inhomogeneous setting to describe non-uniform particle suspensions.
Surprisingly again, we could not find any account of this generalization in the literature. We emphasize however that such an inhomogeneous setting
should also arise naturally from the small-
\operatorname{Wi}
expansion of the inhomogeneous Oldroyd–B model, which is the formal exact closure of the kinetic Hookean dumbbell model.
Note that we are still considering an homogeneous solvent fluid.
At infinite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
, the adaptation is straightforward:
the fluid equations (
4.6
) are simply coupled to a conservation equation for the particle density
\rho:=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n
,
6
6

6

The physical particle density is rather given by
x\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f(x,n)\,\mathrm{d}n
, but for notational convenience we choose to normalize it by the area of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. In particular, we have
\rho\in\frac{1}{\omega_{d}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\rho=0,

while the stress
\sigma
is now a function both of
\rho
and of the Rivlin–Ericksen tensors
\{A_{k}(u)\}_{k}
where non-Newtonian corrections to the pure solvent viscosity
\eta_{0}
are taken proportional to the suspended particle density
\rho
. More precisely, the inhomogeneous second-order fluid model takes the form

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma)+\nabla p=h,\\[2.84526pt]
(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\rho=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\sigma\,=\,(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}\rho)A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}\rho A_{2}(u)+\alpha_{2}\rho A_{1}(u)^{2},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\end{array}\right.

for some coefficients
\eta_{1},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Inhomogeneous versions of higher-order fluid models are formulated similarly.

At finite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
, on the other hand, the particle density
\rho
is no longer simply transported by the fluid, but rather solves a transport-diffusion equation,

(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)\rho=0.

In this diffusive setting, the structure of ordered fluid models becomes slightly more complicated: due to diffusion, the transport-diffusion operator that appears in the Rivlin–Ericksen tensors (
4.18
) at finite Péclet number does not commute with multiplication with the particle density
\rho
. The second-order fluid model then rather takes the form

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma)+\nabla p=h,\\[2.84526pt]
(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)\rho=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\sigma\,=\,(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}\rho)A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+\alpha_{2}\rho A_{1}(u)^{2},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\end{array}\right.

for some coefficients
\eta_{1},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, in terms of the modified inhomogeneous second-order Rivlin–Ericksen tensor

(4.31)

A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}-{\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)(\rho A_{1}(u))+\rho\big((\nabla u)^{T}A_{1}(u)+A_{1}(u)(\nabla u)\big).

Indeed, due to the diffusion, the latter quantity does not reduce to the Rivlin–Ericksen tensor defined in (
4.18
): we have
A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)\neq\rho A_{2}^{\prime}(u)
in general along solutions --- in contrast with the case of infinite Péclet number.
7
7

7

At infinite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
, as the particle density
\rho
satisfies
(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\rho=0
, we indeed obtain
(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)(\rho A_{1}(u))=\rho(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)A_{1}(u)
, so that the system (
4.2.4
) reduces to (
4.2.4
).

This non-standard modified second-order fluid system is exactly the one that we derive from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory, cf. (
1.1
).
Similarly, corresponding modified models can be formulated to higher order: the inhomogeneous third-order model amounts to (
4.2.4
) with stress

\sigma\,=\,(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}\rho)A_{1}(u)+\alpha_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+\alpha_{2}A_{1}(u)^{2}+\beta_{1}A_{3}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+\beta_{1}^{\prime}B_{3}^{\prime}(\rho,u)\\
+\beta_{2}\big(A_{1}(u)A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)A_{1}(u)\big)+\beta_{3}\rho A_{1}(u){\operatorname{tr}}(A_{1}(u)^{2}),

in terms of the modified inhomogeneous third-order Rivlin–Ericksen tensor

A_{3}^{\prime}(\rho,u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+(\nabla u)^{T}A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)+A_{2}^{\prime}(\rho,u)(\nabla u),

and in terms of the following additional quantity, which needs to be included similarly as in (
4.20
) at finite Péclet number,

B_{3}^{\prime}(\rho,u)\,:=\,(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta+u\cdot\nabla)(\rho A_{1}(u)^{2})+\rho\big((\nabla u)^{T}A_{1}(u)^{2}+A_{1}(u)^{2}(\nabla u)\big).

4.2.5.
Well-posedness of ordered fluid models

We focus for shortness on the second-order fluid model.
There has actually been a fair amount of confusion on the relevant range of parameters
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
: the sign condition
\alpha_{1}<0
in (
4.14
) is motivated by experimental normal-stress measurements, but it appears inconsistent with thermodynamics; see e.g.
[
36
]
for a detailed discussion from the physics perspective.
From the mathematical point of view, this inconsistency leads to ill-posedness issues. The matter was investigated by Galdi
[
43
]
, who showed the following for the second-order fluid equations (
4.6
)–(
4.8
) at infinite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
:

—

the local-in-time well-posedness holds whenever
1/\alpha_{1}>-\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}
stands for the Poincaré constant in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, which is quite consistent with the choice (
4.14
) (although the case of a negative
\alpha_{1}
with a small absolute value is prohibited);

—

the long-time well-posedness, as well as the stability of steady solutions, can only hold provided that
\alpha_{1}>0
.

For the corresponding system (
4.19
) with finite Péclet number
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
, the situation is even worse: even local-in-time well-posedness actually fails whenever
\alpha_{1}<0
because the equation then behaves like a backward heat equation.
Yet, even though the kinetic Doi model itself is known to be thermodynamically consistent (see
[
27
, Chapter 8]
), our analysis leads us to a second-order fluid with
\alpha_{1}<0
,
and it is thus crucial to determine what meaning should be given to the model in that case.
In fact, in the small-
\operatorname{Wi}
limit,
\varepsilon:=\operatorname{Wi}\ll 1
we find

(4.32)

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(\varepsilon\gamma_{1},\varepsilon\gamma_{2}),\qquad\text{for some $\gamma_{1}<0,\penalty\ \gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}$.}

In this perturbative setting
\varepsilon\ll 1
,
although the equation is ill-posed for fixed
\varepsilon
,
there are several ways to rearrange the nonlinearity and define well-posed notions of approximate solutions that only satisfy the system (
4.19
) up to a higher-order
O(\varepsilon^{2})
remainder. A hierarchical way has been formulated in Proposition
2.4
.
There are also non-hierarchical ways to perturbatively make sense of the ill-posed second-order fluid model (
4.19
), which may be more desirable in particular for stability issues. We address this aspect in Appendix
B
.

4.2.6.
Non-Newtonian properties of hydrodynamic approximation

We comment on the rheological features of the second-order fluid system (
1.1
) with explicit parameters (
2.8
), which we have derived from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory.
We briefly describe the resulting non-Newtonian properties and how they depend on microscopic features.
We focus here on the physically relevant 3D setting, and
we point out that in the passive case the parameter values agree
with
[
64
, Eqn (41) and Table 2]
and
[
10
, Eqn (7.4)]
.

\bullet

Effective spatial diffusion:

The spatial diffusion
\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}
of the suspended particle density is enhanced by particle activity even at infinite Péclet number: it is replaced by
\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}+\varepsilon\mu_{0}
with
\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{6}U_{0}^{2}
. This naturally follows from the coupling of particle swimming velocities with their rotational diffusion. This phenomenon of increased mixing has been observed in studies such as
[
100
,
99
]
.

\bullet

Modified zero-shear viscosity:

The presence of suspended particles leads to a non-trivial contribution to the zero-shear viscosity: in the homogeneous setting
\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\equiv\frac{1}{\omega_{d}}
, we obtain a zero-shear viscosity

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{0}\,:=\,\eta_{0}+\tfrac{1}{\omega_{d}}\eta_{1}=1+\tfrac{1}{30}\lambda(2+\theta).

In particular, in case of passive particles, this zero-shear viscosity is always larger than the plain fluid viscosity,
\tilde{\eta}_{0}>1
, while particle activity can reverse this effect. For a precise description, first recall that
\theta=6+2\alpha\tfrac{|V_{0}|\ell\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}{k_{B}\Theta}
, cf. (
4.5
), where
\alpha
characterizes the swimming mechanism:

—

for passive particles
\alpha=0
, the zero-shear viscosity is
\tilde{\eta}_{0}=1+\frac{4}{15}\lambda>1
;

—

for so-called puller particles
\alpha>0
, the zero-shear viscosity is even larger than for passive particles;

—

for so-called pusher particles
\alpha<0
, the zero-shear viscosity is smaller than for passive particles, and it can even be smaller than the plain fluid viscosity provided that the activity of the particles is strong enough: we find
\tilde{\eta}_{0}<1
if
\alpha<-\frac{4k_{B}\Theta}{|V_{0}|\ell\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}
.

This prediction is consistent with well-known experimental results, see e.g.
[
105
,
93
,
84
]
, and it has been largely confirmed
in the literature
[
55
,
57
,
98
,
97
,
54
,
3
]
. In particular, for E. coli bacteria (a typical pusher particle), we can assess the value of the parameter
\alpha
using the experimental measurements performed in
[
28
]
: this yields
\alpha<-\frac{4k_{B}\Theta}{|V_{0}|\ell\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}
and the experimental findings of
[
42
]
then confirm our prediction that the effective zero-shear viscosity is smaller than the plain fluid viscosity.

\bullet

Normal-stress differences:

In the homogeneous setting
\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\equiv\frac{1}{\omega_{d}}
, we obtain the following values for first and second normal-stress coefficients, as defined in (
4.11
),

\nu_{10}=\tfrac{\theta}{90}\varepsilon\lambda\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nu_{20}=\tfrac{3-\theta}{315}\varepsilon\lambda.

For passive particles (
\theta=6
), we thus obtain
\nu_{10}>0
,
\nu_{20}<0
, and
\nu_{10}/|\nu_{20}|=7
, which agrees with experiments as discussed in Section
4.2.2
. The amplitude of these normal-stress coefficients is even increased in case of puller particles.
In contrast, for pusher particles, normal-stress coefficients are reduced, and a very large activity could even result into opposite effects: we find
\nu_{10}<0
if
\theta<0
,
and
\nu_{20}>0
if
\theta<3
.
This behavior was also predicted in
[
97
,
92
]
, but has yet to be experimentally verified.

\bullet

Elongational viscosity:

In the homogeneous setting
\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\equiv\frac{1}{\omega_{d}}
, in a uniaxial elongational flow in the direction
e_{1}
, that is,
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}=\kappa(x_{1}e_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{2}e_{2}+x_{3}e_{3}))
, we obtain the following value for the elongational viscosity, as defined in (
4.15
),

\eta_{E}=\big(3+\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda(2+\theta)\big)+\kappa\tfrac{1}{140}\varepsilon\lambda(4+\theta).

This shows that passive suspensions (
\theta=6
) lead to a strain-thickening behavior, which is even increased in case of puller particles. In contrast, for pusher particles, the strain-thickening behavior is reduced, and a very large activity could even result in the opposite effect: the system becomes strain-thinning if
\theta<-4
(that is,
\alpha<-\tfrac{5k_{B}\Theta}{|V_{0}|\ell\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}
).

4.2.7.
Next-order hydrodynamic approximation

The small-
\operatorname{Wi}
expansion performed in Theorem
2.5
is easily pursued to higher orders, but we stick to a formal discussion for shortness.
As we show in Appendix
C
, the next-order description of the suspended particle density involves additional nontrivial transport and anisotropic diffusion terms depending on the surrounding fluid flow: we find

(4.33)

(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\big({\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}}+\varepsilon\mu_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{1}\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big)\nabla\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\Big)=\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{2}\,{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

with explicit coefficients

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{U_{0}^{2}}{d(d-1)},

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{(3d+1)U_{0}^{2}}{d(d-1)^{2}(d+2)},

\displaystyle\mu_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{U_{0}^{2}}{2d(d-1)(d+2)}.

Those nontrivial terms, which differ from the inhomogeneous ordered models suggested in Section
4.2.4
, do actually vanish for particles with vanishing swimming velocity (
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
if
U_{0}=0
).
In any case, we note that homogeneous spatial densities are still stable for (
4.33
) to order
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, and we shall henceforth restrict for simplicity to the homogeneous setting,

\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}=\tfrac{1}{\omega_{d}}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

In addition, we shall focus on the case of infinite Péclet number and of vanishing particle swimming velocity,

\operatorname{Pe}=\infty,\qquad U_{0}=0,

as this choice strongly simplifies the macroscopic equations and as it seems anyhow to be the most relevant setting physically, cf. Section
4.1
(recall however that our rigorous results do not hold for
\operatorname{Pe}=\infty
).
In this setting,
we formally derive in Appendix
C
the following third-order fluid equations from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory,

(4.34)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})=0,\end{array}\right.

where the stress is given by

(4.35)

\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}=(1+\eta_{1})A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}A_{2}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{2}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2}\\
\qquad\,+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{1}A_{3}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{2}\big(A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})A_{2}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+A_{2}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big)+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{3}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}){\operatorname{tr}}(A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2})

with explicit coefficients

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lambda\tfrac{1}{2d(d+2)}(\theta+2),

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\lambda\theta\tfrac{1}{4d^{2}(d+2)},

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lambda\tfrac{1}{2d^{2}(d+4)}(\theta+\tfrac{2d}{d+2}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lambda\theta\tfrac{1}{8d^{3}(d+2)},

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\lambda\tfrac{1}{8d^{3}(d+4)}(3\theta+\tfrac{2d}{d+2}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lambda\tfrac{1}{8d^{3}(d+2)^{2}(d+4)(d+6)}\Big(2d(3d^{2}+10d+6)+\theta(d+4)(3d^{2}+11d+12)\Big).

These third-order fluid coefficients coincide in the passive case (
\theta=6
) with those computed by Brenner
[
10
, Eq. (7.4)]
(once the notation is properly identified).
Regarding the non-Newtonian phenomena discussed in Section
4.2.6
above, the main observation is that this third-order fluid model further describes the expected shear-thinning behavior of suspensions. Indeed, the shear-dependent viscosity is given in 3D as follows, cf. Section
4.2.2
,

\displaystyle\kappa\penalty\ \penalty\ \mapsto\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle 1+\eta_{1}+2\varepsilon^{2}(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})\kappa^{2}

\displaystyle\,\qquad\qquad=\penalty\

\displaystyle 1+\lambda\tfrac{\theta+2}{30}-\varepsilon^{2}\lambda\tfrac{19\theta-12}{18900}\kappa^{2},

which is decreasing in
\kappa
if and only if
\theta>\frac{12}{19}
.
As expected, this shows that passive suspensions (
\theta=6
) lead to a shear-thinning behavior, which is even increased in case of puller particles. In contrast, for pusher particles, the shear-thinning behavior is reduced, and a very large activity could even result in the opposite effect: the system becomes shear-thickening if
\theta<\frac{12}{19}
(that is,
\alpha<-\frac{51k_{B}\Theta}{19|V_{0}|\ell\mu_{\operatorname{fl}}}
).
This possible shear-thickening effect was indeed measured experimentally in
[
42
,
84
]
for suspensions of E. coli bacteria (pusher-type microswimmers) with strong enough activity.
We also refer to
[
57
,
54
,
92
]
for analytical and numerical results showing the same effect.

For active particles with
U_{0}\neq 0
, we have already mentioned that the transport-diffusion equation for the particle density suggested in Section
4.2.4
actually fails at order
O(\varepsilon^{2})
and is to be replaced by the much more complicated equation (
4.33
).
In fact, we show in Appendix
C
that the effect of particle swimming is even worse: the resulting fluid equation for
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
would differ from the third-order fluid model (
4.34
)–(
4.35
) even at uniform particle density and at infinite Péclet number. In particular, an additional dispersive correction
-\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{4}\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
would appear in the fluid equations. We skip the details as the case
|U_{0}|\ll 1
seems to be the most physically relevant anyway.

5.
Conclusion

Starting from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley system (
1.3
), which is a multiscale kinetic model for suspensions of (passive or active) Brownian rigid particles, we have rigorously derived in this work second-ordered fluid models in the regime of a small Weissenberg number
\operatorname{Wi}=\varepsilon\ll 1
.
In the following concluding remarks, we highlight some important aspects of our results.

\bullet

Second-order fluid models are nonlinear macroscopic fluid equations. They have the benefit over multiscale models that their rheological properties are easier to “read off” from the parameters and therefore to compare to experiments. Moreover, since they are lower-dimensional models (the particle orientation is eliminated as a variable), they are more accessible to numerical simulations.

\bullet

The regime of a small Weissenberg number is generally characterized by a small relaxation timescale compared to the convection timescale. For the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model, the relaxation mechanism is orientational Brownian motion. Hence, the relaxation timescale is the inverse of the rotational diffusion constant.
As discussed in Section
4.1
, the regime of a small Weissenberg number is therefore highly relevant for applications with very small particles.

\bullet

The regime of a small Weissenberg number corresponds to weak non-Newtonian effects, which are then naturally captured in form of an
\varepsilon
-expansion. Upon truncation, this formally leads to so-called ordered fluid models: the
k
-th-order fluid model amounts to truncating
O(\varepsilon^{k})
effects.
In this work, we (mainly) focus on the first nontrivial term in the expansion, that is, second-order fluids.

\bullet

Second-order fluid models can exhibit the following well-known rheological properties: zero-shear viscosity different from the solvent viscosity, normal-stress differences, strain-thinning (or thickening), and retardation phase shift in oscillatory flows. Third-order fluids can further exhibit shear-thinning (or thickening). All those non-Newtonian properties are determined quantitatively by the parameters in ordered fluid models.

\bullet

Our rigorous derivation from Doi–Saintillan–Shelley theory comes together with explicit formulas for ordered fluid parameters, which
are in full accordance with previous formal computations for passive suspensions. Moreover, both for active and passive suspensions, our findings agree qualitatively with experimental rheological measurements. Also note that our results definitely rule out the validity of the Oldroyd–B model at order
O(\varepsilon)
for suspensions, as it would wrongly predict a vanishing second normal-stress coefficient.

\bullet

Experimentally, most real-life viscoelastic fluids display
a positive first normal-stress coefficient. However, this leads to second-order fluid models that are thermodynamically inconsistent — and mathematically ill-posed. One might thus doubt whether those models have any reasonable meaning. Yet, in the regime of a small Weissenberg number, we propose a way to resolve this apparent issue: in a nutshell,
while second-order models neglect
O(\varepsilon^{2})
effects anyway, they can be rearranged up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
errors into actual well-posed models.
Hence, while one might argue that there are no real-world fluids that are exactly described by second-order fluid models, the latter still constitute very effective and meaningful approximate models for
\varepsilon\ll 1
.

\bullet

Our results are limited by the technical necessity to include spatial diffusion
\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}>0
. Even though we could allow for a spatial diffusion that is rather weak at small particle volume fraction, cf. (
2.9
), our requirements on
\operatorname{Pe}
are outside of the range for typical applications (see discussion in Section
4.1.1
).
In addition, since our analysis requires strong solutions, we are restricted to the 2D Navier–Stokes case or the 3D Stokes case.

Appendix A
Well-posedness of the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley system

This appendix is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.1
on the well-posedness of the Cauchy problem (
1.3
)–(
1.6
) for the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley system. Let us assume that
h=0
for simplicity, as well as
\mathrm{Pe}=\lambda=\theta=\varepsilon=1
, since those parameters play no role in the analysis.
We then omit the subscript
\varepsilon
in the notation and we set
(u_{\varepsilon},f_{\varepsilon})\equiv(u,f)
.
We focus on the Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
in dimension
d=3
, but standard adaptations of the proof allow to treat the 2D Navier–Stokes case without important additional difficulty.
For a given distribution function
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we shall use the short-hand notation

\displaystyle\rho_{f}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f(\cdot,n)\,\mathrm{d}n.

We split the proof into four main steps.

Step
1. A priori energy estimates: we show that a smooth solution
(u,f)
of the system (
1.3
) satisfies for all
t\geq 0
,

(A.1)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle e^{Ct}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

(A.2)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\|\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle e^{Ct}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

(A.3)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{n}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{t}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\exp\Big(te^{Ct}\big(1+\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}\big)\Big)\|f^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

On the one hand, testing the equation for the fluid velocity
u
with
u
itself, using the incompressibility constraint, and inserting the form of
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
, we find

\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\nabla u:n\otimes n)^{2}f\,=\,-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla u:\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{3}\operatorname{Id}\big)f,

and since the second left-hand side term is nonnegative,

(A.4)

\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

On the other hand, testing the kinetic equation for the particle density
f
with
f
itself, using again the incompressibility constraint, and integrating by parts,
we find

(A.5)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla_{n}f\cdot\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)nf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|f|^{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)n\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}.

By Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality, we can estimate the last factor as

\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{H^{1}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\lesssim\,\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Inserting this into (
A.5
), and appealing to Young’s inequality to absorb the norm of
\nabla_{x}f
, we deduce

(A.6)

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\big(\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}\big)\|f\|^{2}_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

Moreover, integrating the equation for
f
with respect to the angular variable, we get

\partial_{t}\rho_{f}+u\cdot\nabla\rho_{f}-\Delta\rho_{f}+U_{0}\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}nf\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)\,=\,0,

and thus, testing this equation with
\rho_{f}
itself and using the incompressibility constraint,

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,=\,U_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}nf\cdot\nabla\rho_{f}\,\lesssim\,\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\rho_{f}|\nabla\rho_{f}|,

hence

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\|\rho_{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

By Grönwall’s inequality, this proves (
A.2
), and the claim (
A.1
) then follows after combination with (
A.4
). Further combining with (
A.6
) and appealing again to Grönwall’s inequality, the claim (
A.3
) also follows.

Step
2. Construction of approximate solutions.

In order to prove the existence of a weak solution for the system (
1.3
),
we argue by means of a Galerkin approximation method.
More precisely, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the following orthogonal projection on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
,

\displaystyle P_{k}\,:\,\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\,\twoheadrightarrow\,F_{k}\,:=\,\big\{u\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}):\hat{u}(l)=0\text{ for all $|l|>k$}\big\},

where
\{\hat{u}(l)\}_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}
stands for the Fourier coefficients of a periodic function
u\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
.
For all
u\in F_{k}
and
s\geq 0
, we obviously have

(A.7)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\,\leq\,\langle k\rangle^{s}\|u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

Given an initial condition
f^{\circ}\in H^{1}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2})
, we shall consider the following approximate system,

(A.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta u_{k}+\nabla p_{k}=P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[f])+P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f_{k},\nabla P_{k}u_{k}]),\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}f_{k}+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((u_{k}+U_{0}n)f_{k}\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla P_{k}u_{k})nf_{k}\big)=\Delta_{x}f_{k}+\Delta_{n}f_{k},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u_{k})=0,\quad\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u_{k}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
f_{k}|_{t=0}=f^{\circ},\end{array}\right.

and we claim that this system admits a weak solution
(u_{k},f_{k})
with

(A.9)

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3}),

(A.10)

\displaystyle f_{k}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})).

To prove this, we argue by means of a Schauder fixed-point argument and we split the proof into four further substeps.

Substep
2.1. Fixed-point problem.

Given
T>0
, let

E\,:=\,\Big\{v\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3}):{\operatorname{div}}(v)=0,\penalty\ \textstyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}v=0\Big\},

and for all
v\in E
define
\Lambda_{k}(v):=u
as the unique solution of

(A.11)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta u+\nabla p=P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[g])+P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[g,\nabla P_{k}u]),\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}g+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((v+U_{0}n)g\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla P_{k}v)ng\big)=\Delta_{x}g+\Delta_{n}g,\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u)=0,\quad\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u=0,\\[2.84526pt]
g|_{t=0}=f^{\circ}.\end{array}\right.

Note that we use the velocity field
u
and not
v
in the viscous stress
\sigma_{2}
in the equation for
u
so as to preserve its dissipative structure.
The existence of a weak solution
u_{k}
for the approximate system (
A.8
) amounts to finding a fixed point
\Lambda_{k}(u_{k})=u_{k}
.

Before going on with the fixed-point problem, we first check that the above system (
A.11
) is indeed well-posed and defines a map
\Lambda_{k}:E\to E
.
First, given
v\in E
,
by standard parabolic theory,
the above kinetic equation for
g
admits a unique weak solution

g\,\in\,\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\operatorname{L}^{2}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})).

Moreover, the a priori estimates of Step 1 can be repeated to the effect of

(A.12)

\displaystyle\|\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle e^{CT}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{x}g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{n}g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\exp\Big(CT\big(1+\|\nabla v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}\big)\Big)\|f^{\circ}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

To ensure the well-posedness of the elliptic equation for
u
in (
A.11
), we first need to check that
\rho_{g}
has some additional regularity.
Integrating the equation for
g
with respect to the angular variable, we find the following equation for
\rho_{g}
,

\partial_{t}\rho_{g}+v\cdot\nabla\rho_{g}-\Delta\rho_{g}+U_{0}\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}ng\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)=0,

and thus, testing the equation with
\Delta\rho_{g}
, we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}(v\cdot\nabla\rho_{g})\Delta\rho_{g}-U_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}(\Delta\rho_{g})\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}ng\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{x}g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

By Ladyzhenskaya’s and Poincaré’s inequalities, the first right-hand side term can be bounded by

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{7}{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{\frac{7}{4}},

and thus, inserting this into the above, recalling
\|\nabla^{2}\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}
, and using Young’s inequality, we are led to

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{8}\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{x}g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

By Grönwall’s inequality, this implies

\|\nabla\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\Delta\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\Big(\|\nabla\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}g\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\Big)\exp\Big(C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{8}\Big).

Combined with (
A.12
), this shows that
\rho_{g}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
. In particular, we infer
\rho_{g}(t)\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
With this additional regularity result for
\rho_{g}
, we can now finally ensure the well-posedness of the elliptic equation for
u
in (
A.11
). Indeed, for all
t\in[0,T]
, the weak formulation of this equation for
u(t)
can be written as follows: for all
w\in E
,

\displaystyle B_{t,k,g}(w,u(t))\,:=\,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla w:\nabla u(t)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big(n\otimes n:\nabla P_{k}w\big)\big(n\otimes n:\nabla P_{k}u(t)\big)\,g(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\,=\,-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla w\cdot P_{k}\sigma_{1}[g(t)].

For almost all
t
, as
\rho_{g}(t)\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, we note that
B_{t,k,g}
is a coercive continuous bilinear functional. As in addition we have
\|P_{k}\sigma_{1}[g(t)]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\rho_{g}(t)\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}
, we can appeal to the Lax–Milgram theorem and deduce that there exists a unique solution
u\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
of (
A.11
). Further recalling (
A.12
), it satisfies

(A.13)

\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|\rho_{g}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,e^{CT}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

Letting
\Lambda_{k}(v):=u
be the solution of (
A.11
), this shows that we are indeed led to a well-defined map
\Lambda_{k}:E\to E
.

Substep
2.2. Proof that the map
\Lambda_{k}:E\to E
is compact.

Let
(v_{r})_{r}
be a bounded sequence in
E
, and for all
r
let
(u_{r}:=\Lambda_{k}v_{r},g_{r})
be the corresponding solution of the system (
A.11
) with
v
replaced by
v_{r}
.
By (
A.13
), the sequence
(u_{r})_{r}
is bounded in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
.
By the Aubin–Lions lemma, in order to prove that it is actually precompact in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
,
it suffices to check that
(u_{r})_{r}
is also bounded in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
and that
(\partial_{t}u_{r})_{r}
is bounded for instance in
\operatorname{L}^{8/3}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
.
On the one hand, noting that by definition we have
u_{r}=P_{k}u_{r}
,
and appealing to (
A.7
),
we directly find

\|u_{r}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}\,=\,\|P_{k}u_{r}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{k}\,\|u_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}},

which shows that
(u_{r})_{r}
is indeed also bounded in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
.
We turn to the boundedness of time derivatives.
Taking the time derivative of the elliptic equation for
u_{r}
, we find

-\Delta(\partial_{t}u_{r})-P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[g_{r},\nabla P_{k}(\partial_{t}u_{r})])+\nabla(\partial_{t}p_{r})\\
\,=\,P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[\partial_{t}g_{r}])+P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[\partial_{t}g_{r},\nabla P_{k}u_{r}]).

Arguing as for (
A.13
), we deduce

(A.14)

\|\partial_{t}u_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|P_{k}\sigma_{1}[\partial_{t}g_{r}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|P_{k}\sigma_{2}[\partial_{t}g_{r},\nabla P_{k}u_{r}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

To estimate the two right-hand side terms, we recall the definition of
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
, we insert the equation for
g_{r}
, we integrate by parts in the
n
-integrals, and we appeal to (
A.7
) again. For instance, for the term involving
\sigma_{1}
, we find

\displaystyle\|P_{k}\sigma_{1}[\partial_{t}g_{r}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\theta\Big\|P_{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)\Big({\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((v_{r}+U_{0}n)g_{r}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla P_{k}v_{r})ng_{r}\big)-\Delta_{x}g_{r}-\Delta_{n}g_{r}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}n\Big\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\theta\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{3}P_{k}\nabla_{x_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)\big((v_{r}+U_{0}n)g_{r}\big)_{i}\,\mathrm{d}n

\displaystyle\qquad-P_{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla_{n}(n\otimes n)\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla P_{k}v_{r})ng_{r}\,\mathrm{d}n

\displaystyle\qquad-P_{k}\Delta_{x}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big(n\otimes n-\tfrac{1}{d}\operatorname{Id}\big)\,g_{r}\,\mathrm{d}n-P_{k}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Delta_{n}(n\otimes n)\,g_{r}\,\mathrm{d}n\bigg\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k}

\displaystyle\|v_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}}\|g_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}+\|g_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}.

Noting that we have
\|P_{k}(h_{1}P_{k}h_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\leq\|(P_{2k}h_{1})(P_{k}h_{2})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}
for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
,
we can argue similarly to estimate the second term in (
A.14
). Further using Jensen’s inequality, we are led to

\|\partial_{t}u_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{k}\,(1+\|u_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}})(1+\|v_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}})\|g_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}.

By Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality, this yields

\|\partial_{t}u_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{k}\,(1+\|u_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}})(1+\|v_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}})\|g_{r}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|g_{r}\|_{H^{1}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

The a priori estimate (
A.12
) for
g=g_{r}
then ensures that the sequence
(\partial_{t}u_{r})_{r}
is bounded in
\operatorname{L}^{8/3}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
,
and the compactness of
\Lambda_{k}
follows.

Substep
2.3. Proof that the map
\Lambda_{k}:E\to E
is continuous.

Given a sequence
(v_{r})_{r}
that converges (strongly) to some
v
in
E
,
we need to check that the image
u_{r}:=\Lambda_{k}v_{r}
converges to
\Lambda_{k}v
.
By compactness of
\Lambda_{k}
, we already know that up to a subsequence
u_{r}=\Lambda_{k}v_{r}
converges (strongly) to some
w
in
E
, and it remains to show that it satisfies
w=\Lambda_{k}v
. By definition of
\Lambda_{k}
, we recall that
u_{r}
satisfies the system (
A.11
) with
v
replaced by
v_{r}
, and we denote by
g_{r}
the corresponding density. Using the strong convergence of
u_{r}
and
v_{r}
along the extracted subsequence, recalling the a priori estimates for
g_{r}
, cf. (
A.12
), and using weak compactness for
g_{r}
, we can pass to the limit in the weak formulation of this system. This shows that the limit
w
satisfies the system defining
\Lambda_{k}v
, hence we have
w=\Lambda_{k}v
by uniqueness.

Substep
2.4. Application of Schauder’s fixed-point theorem.

Recall the a priori estimate (
A.13
), that is,

\|\Lambda_{k}v\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}H^{1}_{x}}\,\leq\,Ce^{CT}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

Considering the following non-empty closed convex subset of
E
,

K\,:=\,\big\{u\in E:\|u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{T}H^{1}_{x}}\leq Ce^{CT}\|\rho_{f^{\circ}}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\big\},

the restriction of
\Lambda_{k}
defines a map
\Lambda_{k}|_{K}:K\to K
that is compact and continuous by Steps 2.2 and 2.3.
By Schauder’s theorem, this map must then admit a fixed point
u_{k}\in K
, that is,
u_{k}=\Lambda_{k}(u_{k})
. Combined with the a priori estimates (
A.12
), this concludes the proof of the existence of a weak solution of the approximate system (
A.8
) satisfying (
A.9
).

Step
3. Existence of a weak solution for the system (
1.3
).

We argue by means of a Galerkin method based on the approximations defined in Step 2.
For that purpose, given an initial condition
f^{\circ}\in\operatorname{L}^{2}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
, we start by considering an approximating sequence of smooth initial conditions
(f^{\circ}_{k})_{k}\subset C^{\infty}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
that converges strongly to
f^{\circ}
in
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
.
By Step 2, for all
k
, we may then consider the solution
(u_{k},f_{k})
of the approximate system (
A.8
) with initial condition
f_{k}^{\circ}
, that is,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta u_{k}+\nabla p_{k}=P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[f])+P_{k}{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f_{k},\nabla P_{k}u_{k}]),\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}f_{k}+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((u_{k}+U_{0}n)f_{k}\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla P_{k}u_{k})nf_{k}\big)=\Delta_{x}f_{k}+\Delta_{n}f_{k},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(u_{k})=0,\quad\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u_{k}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
f_{k}|_{t=0}=f_{k}^{\circ},\end{array}\right.

with

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3}),

\displaystyle f_{k}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\operatorname{L}^{2}\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})).

From Step 2, we further learn that
(u_{k},f_{k})
is uniformly bounded in these spaces.
By weak compactness, we deduce that up to a subsequence
u_{k}
converges weakly-* to some
u
in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
, and that
f_{k}
converges weakly-* to some
f
in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))
and weakly in
\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))
.
In addition, examining the equation satisfied by
f_{k}
, a simple argument allows to check that
\partial_{t}f_{k}
is bounded e.g. in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T;W^{-2,1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}))
, so that the Aubin–Lions lemma further entails that
f_{k}
converges strongly to
f
in
\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T;\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3})
.
These convergences now allow to pass to the limit in the weak formulation of the system, and we easily conclude that the extracted limit
(u,f)
satisfies the limiting system (
1.3
).

Step
4. Weak-strong uniqueness for the system (
1.3
).

Let
(u,f)
and
(u^{\prime},f^{\prime})
be two weak solutions of (
1.3
) with common initial condition

f|_{t=0}=f^{\prime}|_{t=0}=f^{\circ},

and assume that
(u^{\prime},f^{\prime})
further satisfies

(A.15)

\displaystyle u^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{2}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})^{3}),

\displaystyle f^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})).

Consider the differences
U=u-u^{\prime}
and
F=f-f^{\prime}
. On the one hand, the equations for
u
and
u^{\prime}
yield

\displaystyle-\Delta U+\nabla P

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[F])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla u]-\sigma_{2}[f^{\prime},\nabla u^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[F])+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[f,\nabla U]+\sigma_{2}[F,\nabla u^{\prime}]).

Testing this equation with
U
itself, using the incompressibility constraint, and taking advantage of the additional dissipation given by
\sigma_{2}
, we get

(A.16)

\displaystyle\|\nabla U\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{1}[F]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\sigma_{2}[F,\nabla u^{\prime}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big(1+\|\nabla u^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}\big)\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}.

On the other hand, the equations for
f
and
f^{\prime}
yield

\partial_{t}F+{\operatorname{div}}_{x}\big((u+U_{0}n)F\big)+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)nF\big)-\Delta_{x}F-\Delta_{n}F\\
\,=\,-{\operatorname{div}}_{x}(Uf^{\prime})-{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla U)nf^{\prime}\big).

Testing this equation with
F
itself, and integrating by parts, we get

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|F\|^{2}_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{x}F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{n}F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|F|^{2}{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u)n\big)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla_{x}F\cdot Uf^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\nabla_{n}F\cdot\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla U)nf^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{x}F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\|U\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x,n}}+\|\nabla_{n}F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\|\nabla U\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x,n}}.

By Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality, the first right-hand side term can be bounded by

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{4}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{H^{1}_{x}\operatorname{L}^{2}_{n}}^{\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{x}F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Inserting this into the above and appealing to Young’s and Poincaré’s inequalities, we are led to

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|F\|^{2}_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\,\lesssim\,\big(\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}\big)\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}+\|\nabla U\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\|f^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x,n}}^{2},

and thus, recalling (
A.16
),

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|F\|^{2}_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}\,\lesssim\,\Big(\big(1+\|\nabla u\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{4}\big)+\big(1+\|\nabla u^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}\big)\|f^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{x,n}}^{2}\Big)\|F\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x,n}}^{2}.

By the assumed regularity of
u,u^{\prime},f^{\prime}
, this implies
F=0
by Grönwall’s inequality, hence also
U=0
by (
A.16
).
∎

Appendix B
Perturbative well-posedness of ordered fluid equations

This appendix is devoted to the perturbative well-posedness of the second-order fluid model (
1.1
) that we derive from the Doi–Saintillan–Shelley model. We recall that this model is a priori ill-posed as it behaves like a backward heat equation, but we show that well-posed notions of
approximate
solutions can be defined.
We start below with the proof of Proposition
2.4
on so-called approximate hierarchical solutions, which are motivated by the formal
\varepsilon
-expansion.

Next, we show that
there are also alternative, non-hierarchical ways to perturbatively make sense of (
1.1
), which may be more desirable in particular for stability issues. For shortness, we shall focus here on
homogeneous
second-order fluids at
\operatorname{Pe}<\infty
as defined in Section
4.2.3
:
more precisely, instead of (
1.1
), we consider (
4.19
), that is,

(B.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\\[2.84526pt]
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\eta_{0}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{2}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})=0.\end{array}\right.

Comparing to corresponding ill-posedness issues in the Boussinesq theory for water waves, we recall that there is a standard way to rearrange the ill-posed Boussinesq equation perturbatively and make it well-posed, see
[
14
]
: in a nutshell, the idea is to replace indefinite operators like
1+\varepsilon\Delta
by corresponding positive operators like
(1-\varepsilon\Delta)^{-1}
up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
errors.
We notice that a similar so-called
Boussinesq trick
can be used in the present situation as well: for any value of
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
, both at finite and infinite Péclet number, it leads us to a perturbative rearrangement of the second-order fluid equation that is well-posed and indeed equivalent to (
B.1
) up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
terms.
The so-defined solution is easily checked to differ from the corresponding hierarchical solution only by
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.
We focus here on the relevant ill-posed case
\gamma_{1}\leq 0
.
Note that this procedure is easily extended to higher-order fluid equations (see also
[
2
,
35
]
in a different context).

Proposition B.1

(Boussinesq-like solutions)
.

Consider the system (
B.1
) in the regime
\varepsilon\ll 1
with parameters
\eta_{0},\operatorname{Pe}>0
,
\gamma_{1}\leq 0
, and
\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
,
d\leq 3
:

Given
s>\frac{d}{2}
,
T_{0}>0
, and
h\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})\cap W^{1,\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
, provided that
\varepsilon\ll 1
is small enough (depending on
s,T_{0},h
and on all parameters), the following nonlinear problem admits a unique solution
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d})
,

(B.2)

\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=\big(1-\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta)\big)h+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})),\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}=0,\end{array}\right.

where we have set for abbreviation

(B.3)

\qquad F_{0}(u)\,:=\,\gamma_{1}(u\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(u)+\gamma_{1}\big((\nabla u)^{T}2\!\operatorname{D}(u)+2\!\operatorname{D}(u)(\nabla u)\big)+\gamma_{2}(2\!\operatorname{D}(u))^{2}.

Moreover, the so-defined solution
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
B.1
) with
\operatorname{Re}=0
for some controlled error term
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

(ii)

Navier–Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=1
,
d=2
:

Further assume
\eta_{0}\geq\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}
.
8
8

8

For
\eta_{0}<\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}
, the equations would need to be rearranged differently; we skip it here for shortness.

Given
s>1
,
T_{0}>0
,
u^{\circ}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{2}
with
{\operatorname{div}}(u^{\circ})=0
, and
h\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T_{0};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{2})
, provided that
\varepsilon\ll 1
is small enough (depending on
s,T_{0},u^{\circ},h
and on all parameters), the following nonlinear problem admits a unique solution
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{2})\cap\operatorname{L}^{2}(0,T_{0};H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{2})
,

(B.4)

\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}(\eta_{0}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}\\
\hskip 142.26378pt\,=\,(1+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}\Delta)h+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})),\\
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})=0,\\
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=u^{\circ},\end{array}\right.

where we have set for abbreviation

\qquad F_{1}(u)\,:=\,2\gamma_{1}(\nabla u)^{T}(\nabla u)+\gamma_{2}(2\!\operatorname{D}(u))^{2}.

Moreover, the so-defined solution
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies (
B.1
) with
\operatorname{Re}=1
with some controlled error term
O(\varepsilon^{2})
and with initial condition
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=u^{\circ}
.∎

B.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.4

Let
(u_{0},\rho_{0})
and
(u_{1},\rho_{1})
satisfy the systems (
2.6
) and (
2.7
), respectively. Their superposition
(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon})=(u_{0}+\varepsilon u_{1},\rho_{0}+\varepsilon\rho_{1})
is easily checked to satisfy the non-homogeneous second-order fluid equations (
1.1
) in the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon},\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}-(\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}+\varepsilon\mu_{0})\Delta\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon},\\[2.84526pt]
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon})A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon},\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{2}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2},\\[2.84526pt]
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})=0,\\[2.84526pt]
(\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon},\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})|_{t=0}=(\rho^{\circ},u^{\circ}),\end{array}\right.

with explicit remainders given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\lambda\theta\tfrac{\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)}{\operatorname{div}}(\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(u_{1}))

\displaystyle-\gamma_{1}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}\big(A_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon})-A_{2}^{\prime}(u_{0},\rho_{0})\big)-\lambda\theta\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d^{2}(d+4)}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}{\operatorname{div}}\big(\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2}-\rho_{0}\operatorname{D}(u_{0})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle-\lambda\tfrac{2\omega_{d}}{d(d+2)(d+4)}{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\rho_{0}\big(\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\operatorname{D}(u_{1})+\operatorname{D}(u_{1})\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big)+2\rho_{1}\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Re}(u_{1}\cdot\nabla)u_{1}-{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[\rho_{1}+g_{1},\nabla u_{1}]),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{d(d-1)}U_{0}^{2}\Delta\rho_{1}+u_{1}\cdot\nabla\rho_{1}.

In the proof of Proposition
3.2
, we recall that we have actually shown that after elimination of
g_{1},g_{2}
the solutions
(u_{0},\rho_{1})
and
(u_{1},\rho_{2})
of the hierarchy (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) precisely satisfy equations (
2.6
)–(
2.7
) (for a specific set of parameters).
Hence, the well-posedness of hierarchical solutions of (
2.6
)–(
2.7
) follows from the proof of Proposition
3.1
, which indeed concerns the well-posedness of the hierarchy (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) in the Stokes case
\operatorname{Re}=0
. The proof is analogous in the 2D Navier–Stokes case and is skipped for conciseness.
∎

B.2.
Proof of Proposition
B.1
in Stokes case

Let
\operatorname{Re}=0
and
d\leq 3
.
We split the proof into three steps, first deriving the suitable reformulation of the equations, and then proving the existence and uniqueness of smooth solutions.

Step
1. Reformulation.

Equation (
B.2
) implies in particular

h\,=\,-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})h+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon},

and therefore, using this to replace
\varepsilon\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})h
in the right-hand side of (
B.2
),

-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))\\
-\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Big(-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})h\Big).

Recalling the definition of
F_{0}
, cf. (
B.3
), and reorganizing
O(\varepsilon)
terms, we precisely deduce the second-order fluid equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=h+\varepsilon^{2}R_{\varepsilon},\\[2.84526pt]
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}=\eta_{0}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{2}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2},\end{array}\right.

for some modified pressure field
\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and some remainder term

R_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Big({\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))-\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})h\Big).

Step
2. Existence.

Let
s>\frac{d}{2}
be fixed.
We proceed by an iterative scheme.
We set
\bar{u}_{0}:=0
and for all
n\geq 0
, given
\bar{u}_{n}\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
, we define
\bar{u}_{n+1}\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
as the unique solution of the linear problem

(B.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{n+1}-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big((\bar{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n+1})\big)+\nabla\bar{p}_{n+1}\\
\hskip 142.26378pt=\big(1-\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\big)h+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n})),\\
{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{u}_{n+1})=0,\end{array}\right.

where we have set

G_{0}(u)\,:=\,\gamma_{1}\big((\nabla u)^{T}2\!\operatorname{D}(u)+2\!\operatorname{D}(u)(\nabla u)\big)+\gamma_{2}(2\!\operatorname{D}(u))^{2}.

We split the proof into two further substeps.

Substep
2.1. Sobolev a priori estimates.

Applying
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}=(1-\Delta)^{s/2}
to both sides of the above equation, testing it with
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\bar{u}_{n+1}
, and using the incompressibility constraint, we find

\eta_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\bar{u}_{n+1}|^{2}\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\bar{u}_{n+1}\cdot\big(1-\varepsilon\tfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\eta_{0}}(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\big)\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}h\\
-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\bar{u}_{n+1}:\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n})-2\varepsilon\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\operatorname{D}(\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\bar{u}_{n+1}):[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},\bar{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla]\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n+1}),

hence, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

(B.6)

\|\nabla\bar{u}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n})\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\\
+\varepsilon\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},\bar{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla]\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n+1})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

To estimate the last term, we use the following form of the Kato–Ponce commutator estimate
[
75
, Lemma X1]
: for all
u,v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
and all
p,q\in[2,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
, we have

(B.7)

\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u\cdot\nabla]\!\operatorname{D}(v)\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|u\|_{W^{s,q}_{x}}\|v\|_{W^{2,p}_{x}}+\|u\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{x}}\|v\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}},

and thus, properly choosing
p,q
and appealing to the Sobolev embedding with
s>\frac{d}{2}
, we deduce

\|[\langle\nabla\rangle^{s},u\cdot\nabla]\!\operatorname{D}(v)\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|u\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\|v\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}.

Using this to estimate the last right-hand side term in (
B.6
),
inserting the definition of
G_{0}
, and further appealing to the Sobolev embedding with
s>\frac{d}{2}
, we are led to

\|\bar{u}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|\bar{u}_{n}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|\bar{u}_{n}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\|\bar{u}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}.

Provided that
\|\bar{u}_{n}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\leq C_{0}
and that
\varepsilon C_{0}\ll_{s}1
is small enough, the last right-hand side term can be absorbed and we are led to

\|\bar{u}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon C_{0}^{2}.

Choosing
C_{0}:=C_{s}(\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}})
for some
C_{s}\gg_{s}1
large enough, we then deduce, provided that
\varepsilon C_{0}\ll_{s}1
is small enough,

\|\bar{u}_{n}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\qquad\implies\qquad\|\bar{u}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\leq\,C_{0}.

Recalling the choice
\bar{u}_{0}=0
, this proves by induction that we have for all
n\geq 0
, provided that
\varepsilon\ll_{s,h}1
is small enough,

(B.8)

\|\bar{u}_{n}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\simeq_{s}\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}.

Substep
2.2. Contraction.

The difference
\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n}
satisfies

-\eta_{0}\Delta(\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n})+\nabla(\bar{p}_{n+1}-\bar{p}_{n})=\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big((\bar{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n})\big)\\
+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(((\bar{u}_{n}-\bar{u}_{n-1})\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n})\big)+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n})-G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n-1})).

Testing this equation with
\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n}
and using the incompressibility constraint, we get similarly as in (
B.6
) above,

\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim\,\varepsilon\|(\bar{u}_{n}-\bar{u}_{n-1})\cdot\nabla\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{n})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n})-G_{0}(\bar{u}_{n-1})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}},

and thus, inserting the definition of
G_{0}
and appealing to the Sobolev embedding with
s>\frac{d}{2}
,

\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\varepsilon\|(\bar{u}_{n-1},\bar{u}_{n})\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{n}-\bar{u}_{n-1})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

Provided that
\varepsilon\ll_{s,h}1
small enough, inserting the a priori estimate (
B.8
), we deduce

\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{n+1}-\bar{u}_{n})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}\,\leq\,\tfrac{1}{2}\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{n}-\bar{u}_{n-1})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}.

Together with the a priori bound (
B.8
), this contraction estimate entails that the sequence
(\bar{u}_{n})_{n}
converges weakly in
H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
to some limit
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
. By the Rellich theorem, recalling
s>\frac{d}{2}
, this allows to pass to the limit in (
B.5
) and to deduce that the limit
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
is a solution of the nonlinear equation (
B.2
). In addition, it automatically satisfies the a priori estimate

(B.9)

\|\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\,\lesssim_{s}\,\|h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}+\varepsilon\|(\partial_{t}-\Delta)h\|_{H^{s-1}_{x}}.

Step
3. Uniqueness.

Let
s>\frac{d}{2}
be fixed.
Let
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{d}
be two solutions of (
B.2
). Their difference then satisfies

-\eta_{0}\Delta(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\big)\\
+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla)2\!\operatorname{D}(\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\big)+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(G_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})-G_{0}(\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})).

Arguing as in Step 2.2 above, we easily deduce

\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,\lesssim\,\varepsilon\|(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{s+1}_{x}}\|\nabla(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}_{\varepsilon})\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

By the a priori estimate (
B.9
) for
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}
, we deduce
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}=\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}
provided that
\varepsilon\ll_{s,h}1
is small enough.∎

B.3.
Proof of Proposition
B.1
in Navier–Stokes case

Let
\operatorname{Re}=1
and
d=2
.
We skip the proof of well-posedness, which is a straightforward modification of the above proof in the corresponding Stokes case.
It remains to derive the suitable reformulation of the equations.
For that purpose, first note that equation (
B.4
) can be rewritten as

(B.10)

(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}(\eta_{0}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}\\
=h+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}(2\operatorname{D}(h))+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(2\operatorname{D}((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big),

where
F_{0}
is defined in (
B.3
).
This yields in particular the following relation,

h\,=\,(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}\\
-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}(\eta_{0}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}(2\operatorname{D}(h))-\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(2\operatorname{D}((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big).

Using this to replace
\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}(2\operatorname{D}(h))
in the right-hand side of (
B.10
), we find after straightforward simplifications

(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\eta_{0}\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))\\
+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\Big[2\operatorname{D}\Big((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}(\eta_{0}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\\
-\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}(2\operatorname{D}(h))-\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(2\operatorname{D}((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big)\Big)\Big].

Recalling the definition of
F_{0}
, cf. (
B.3
), and reorganizing
O(\varepsilon)
terms, we precisely deduce the second-order fluid equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon})+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=h+\varepsilon^{2}R_{\varepsilon},\\
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}=\eta_{0}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{1}A_{2}^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\gamma_{2}A_{1}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})^{2},\end{array}\right.

for some modified pressure field
P_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and some remainder term

R_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,-\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\Big[2\operatorname{D}\Big(\gamma_{1}(\eta_{0}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}})\Delta^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}(2\operatorname{D}(h))+{\operatorname{div}}(F_{0}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))\\
-\gamma_{1}{\operatorname{div}}\big(2\operatorname{D}((\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\big)\Big)\Big].

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Derivation of third-order fluid equations

In this section, we extend Proposition
3.3

and derive the corresponding equations to next order as stated in Section
4.2.7
— we keep the derivation formal and skip detailed error estimates for shortness.
By definition of
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
in Proposition
3.1
, we first note that
(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon})
now satisfies

(C.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{t}+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\Delta\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\bar{p}_{\varepsilon}=h+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{1}[g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{3}])\\
\hskip 142.26378pt+{\operatorname{div}}(\sigma_{2}[\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon g_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{2},\nabla\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}])+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\\
(\partial_{t}-{\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}}\Delta+\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}=-\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}(\varepsilon g_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{2})\rangle+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\end{array}\right.

and it remains to evaluate the different contributions of
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
in the right-hand side.

C.1.
Equation for particle density

Using
n=-\tfrac{1}{d-1}\Delta_{n}n
, we have

\displaystyle\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}(\varepsilon g_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon U_{0}\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}n(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}n\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\tfrac{U_{0}}{d-1}\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}n\Delta_{n}(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}n\Big).

Using the defining equations for
g_{1},g_{2}
, we find

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}(g_{1}+\varepsilon g_{2})

\displaystyle\,=\,U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}\rho_{0}+{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{0})n\rho_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\operatorname{Pe}}\Delta_{x}+u_{0}\cdot\nabla_{x})g_{1}+\varepsilon P_{1}^{\bot}\big(U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}(\rho_{1}+g_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon{\operatorname{div}}_{n}\big(\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{1})n\rho_{0}+\pi_{n}^{\bot}(\nabla u_{0})n(\rho_{1}+g_{1})\big).

Inserting this identity into the above, as well as the explicit expression for
g_{1}
in Proposition
3.1
, then recalling (
3.10
) and (
3.12
), and using integral computations (
3.11
),

\displaystyle\langle U_{0}n\cdot\nabla_{x}(\varepsilon g_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle\,=-\varepsilon\tfrac{U_{0}^{2}}{d(d-1)}\,\Delta\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\quad\ -\varepsilon^{2}\tfrac{2U_{0}^{2}}{d(d-1)(d+2)}\,{\operatorname{div}}\Big(\tfrac{d+1}{d-1}\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\nabla\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\operatorname{div}}(\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\operatorname{D}(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}))\Big)+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Inserting this computation into (
C.1
), we precisely get the claimed equation (
4.33
).

C.2.
Equation for fluid velocity

As the obtained equation (
4.33
) for
\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}
shows that homogeneous spatial densities are stable to order
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, we can focus for simplicity on the homogeneous setting,

\bar{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\,\equiv\,\tfrac{1}{\omega_{d}}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).
